\\\\\nFramework                   & HuggingFace TRL SFTTrainer \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:train_time} reports per-condition training wall-clock times.\nConditions with longer trace inputs (e.g., \\texttt{full\\_trace}, VOTA) require proportionally more training time due to increased sequence lengths.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Training wall-clock time per condition on a single RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU. Where multiple seeds were run, individual times are listed.}\n\\label{tab:train_time}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Seed(s)} & \\textbf{Time (h)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nno\\_trace       & 42         & ${\\sim}$0.5 \\\\\nfull\\_trace     & 42         & ${\\sim}$2.2 \\\\\nlabel\\_only     & 42         & ${\\sim}$2.2 \\\\\nVOTA                     & 42         & ${\\sim}$2.3 \\\\\nexception\\_only & 42         & ${\\sim}$2.1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & 123        & ${\\sim}$2.1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & 456        & ${\\sim}$1.3 \\\\\nae\\_only        & 42         & ${\\sim}$1.8 \\\\\nloop\\_only      & 42         & ${\\sim}$2.1 \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae    & 42         & ${\\sim}$1.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{VOTA Extraction Algorithm}\n\\label{sec:vota_algorithm}\n\nAlgorithm~\\ref{alg:vota} describes the three-component VOTA extraction pipeline applied to a raw \\texttt{sys.settrace} execution log.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{VOTA Trace Extraction}\n\\label{alg:vota}\n\\small\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Raw trace $T$ from \\texttt{sys.settrace} (list of events)\n\\ENSURE Compressed VOTA representation $V$\n\\STATE $V \\leftarrow$ empty string\n\\STATE \\textbf{// Component 1: Exception summary}\n\\FOR{each event $e$ in $T$ with type \\texttt{exception}}\n  \\STATE Parse traceback $\\rightarrow$ extract error type, message, file, line\n  \\STATE Append \\texttt{[ERROR] type: message (file:line)} to $V$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{no exception events found}\n  \\STATE Append \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} to $V$\n\\ENDIF\n\\STATE \\textbf{// Component 2: Assertion-point comparison}\n\\FOR{each \\texttt{assert} or \\texttt{return} event $e$ in $T$}\n  \\STATE Extract actual value from execution state\n  \\STATE Extract expected value from test assertion\n  \\STATE Append \\texttt{[ASSERT] actual=... expected=... match=T/F} to $V$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\STATE \\textbf{// Component 3: Loop compression}\n\\FOR{each consecutive repeated line sequence $S$ in $T$}\n  \\IF{$|S| \\geq 2$ iterations of same loop body}\n    \\STATE Replace $S$ with single iteration + \\texttt{[repeated $\\times$N]}\n  \\ENDIF\n\\ENDFOR\n\\STATE Append compressed non-exception lines to $V$\n\\RETURN $V$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{VOTA Trace Examples}\n\\label{sec:vota_examples}\n\nWe provide two representative VOTA trace examples illustrating the fail and pass cases.\nIn the fail case, the exception event provides an unambiguous fault signal.\nIn the pass case, the absence of an exception event combined with a matching assertion point confirms correct execution.\n\n\\paragraph{Fail example.}\nA \\texttt{ZeroDivisionError} triggers an \\texttt{[ERROR]} event, providing a direct fault signal that is sufficient for the model to predict \\texttt{fail}.\n\n\\begin{small}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n## Function\ndef divide(a, b):\n    return a / b\n\n## Test Input\ndivide(1, 0)\n\n## Expected Output\ninf\n\n## VOTA Trace\n[ERROR] ZeroDivisionError: division by zero\n\n## Verdict\nfail\n\\end{verbatim}\n\\end{small}\n\n\\paragraph{Pass example.}\nNo exception is raised, and the assertion point confirms that the actual output matches the expected value.\n\n\\begin{small}\n\\begin{verbatim}\n## Function\ndef add(a, b):\n    return a + b\n\n## Test Input\nadd(2, 3)\n\n## Expected Output\n5\n\n## VOTA Trace\n[LOOP] No loops detected.\n[ASSERT] actual=5, expected=5\n\n## Verdict\npass\n\\end{verbatim}\n\\end{small}\n\nThese examples illustrate the core asymmetry exploited by the \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition: fail cases produce an explicit \\texttt{[ERROR]} event, while pass cases are characterized by its absence.\nThis binary signal (presence vs.\\ absence of an exception) is sufficient for near-perfect verdict prediction.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Full Experimental Results}\n\\label{sec:full_results}\n\nTables~\\ref{tab:full_qwen} and \\ref{tab:full_deepseek} report the complete experimental results across all conditions, models, and seeds.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Full results for Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B across all conditions and seeds. Conditions with a single seed report no variance. \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves 99.83\\% across all three seeds ($\\sigma{=}$0). \\texttt{ae\\_only} achieves 99.64\\% mean ($\\pm$0.49; seed 123 at 99.08\\% is the outlier).}\n\\label{tab:full_qwen}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{S42} & \\textbf{S123} & \\textbf{S456} & \\textbf{Mean $\\pm$ Std} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & 99.83 & 99.83 & 99.83 & 99.83 $\\pm$ 0.00 \\\\\nae\\_only        & 99.92 & 99.08 & 99.92 & 99.64 $\\pm$ 0.49 \\\\\nVOTA                     & 99.41 & 98.66 & 99.08 & 99.05 $\\pm$ 0.38 \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae    & 99.41 & ---   & ---   & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only     & 86.22 & 86.89 & 84.70 & 85.94 $\\pm$ 1.12 \\\\\nloop\\_only      & 85.63 & 85.88 & 86.64 & 86.05 $\\pm$ 0.52 \\\\\nfull\\_trace     & 81.76 & ---   & ---   & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace       & 75.13 & ---   & ---   & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Full results for DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B across all conditions and seeds. \\texttt{exception\\_only} shows bimodal seed behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\\%, while seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (LoRA initialization outlier, fail recall = 70.5\\%).}\n\\label{tab:full_deepseek}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Notes} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & 42  & 99.33 & epoch 1: 99.83 \\\\\nexception\\_only & 456 & 99.33 & CI [98.82, 99.75] \\\\\nexception\\_only & 123 & 91.09 & fail recall = 70.5\\%; CI [89.40, 92.60] \\\\\n\\midrule\nVOTA                     & 42  & 84.45 & \\\\\nno\\_trace       & 42  & 73.36 & \\\\\nfull\\_trace     & 42  & 79.16 & CI [76.80, 81.30] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Tokenizer and Truncation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:tokenizer_analysis}\n\nThe VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}; confounded by tokenizer-driven truncation; 91.7\\% of DeepSeek training samples were affected) is driven by a training-time truncation asymmetry.\nThe DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}929 for Qwen, causing 91.7\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.\nThe training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates trace content, so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the majority of samples.\n\nA post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\\% (95\\% CI [80.75\\%, 85.24\\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class (fail recall = 31.23\\%).\nPer-class analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:per_class}) confirms that DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\\% fail precision but only 45.43\\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \\texttt{pass}, in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\\% fail recall.\n\nSeed robustness diverges across models: Qwen \\texttt{exception\\_only} produces identical 99.83\\% across all three seeds ($\\sigma{=}0$), while DeepSeek shows bimodal behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\\% (fail recall = 100\\%), seed 123 yields 91.09\\% (fail recall = 70.5\\%).\nBecause \\texttt{exception\\_only} test inputs contain 48.99\\% duplicates (607 unique of 1,190; \\S\\ref{sec:dataset}), CIs assume $n{=}1{,}190$ independent trials; under deduplication ($n{=}607$), Qwen's accuracy is 99.67\\% (Wilson CI [98.81, 99.91]).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Irreducible Sample Analysis}\n\\label{sec:irreducible}\n\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition with Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B misclassifies exactly 2 out of 1,190 test samples across all three seeds (42, 123, 456), with 100\\% overlap across seeds.\nBoth samples (IDs 901 and 905) share the following characteristics:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{Function:} \\texttt{check\\_dict\\_case} (HumanEval/95), a function that checks whether all dictionary keys are uniformly upper-case or lower-case strings.\n\\item \\textbf{Ground-truth label:} \\texttt{pass}.\n\\item \\textbf{Predicted label:} \\texttt{fail} (false negative).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-seed consistency:} Both samples are misclassified identically by all three seeds, indicating a systematic rather than stochastic error.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nManual inspection reveals the root cause: \\texttt{check\\_dict\\_case} uses \\texttt{all()} over a generator expression, and Python's \\texttt{all()} short-circuits on the first \\texttt{True} value, raising \\texttt{GeneratorExit} to terminate the generator.\nBecause \\texttt{GeneratorExit} is a \\texttt{BaseException} subclass, the tracer records it as seven \\texttt{[ERROR] GeneratorExit} lines, even though the function executes correctly and the test passes.\nThe model observes these spurious error markers and reasonably but incorrectly predicts \\texttt{fail}.\nThis is not a model failure but a representation ambiguity: the \\texttt{exception\\_only} format cannot distinguish genuine exceptions from benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclasses triggered by Python's iterator protocol.\n\nMost configurations converge to these same 2 errors: Qwen-1.5B, Qwen-7B (3 seeds), CodeLlama-13B (seeds 42/456), and DeepSeek-6.7B (seeds 42/456) all produce error set $\\{901, 905\\}$.\nHowever, CodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\\% accuracy (0/1190 errors), correctly classifying both samples.\nThis demonstrates that 99.83\\% is a near-perfect \\emph{floor} (the convergence attractor for most LoRA initializations) rather than a hard data ceiling: the representation supports perfect accuracy, but most initializations converge to the same 2-error basin.\nThe practical implication is that these errors are high-probability convergence artifacts addressable through seed selection or ensemble methods, not fundamental limitations of the exception-based representation.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Exception Type Accuracy Breakdown}\n\\label{sec:exception_type}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:exception_type} reports \\texttt{exception\\_only} accuracy stratified by exception type on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).\nAll standard exception types (\\texttt{TypeError}, \\texttt{ValueError}, \\texttt{IndexError}, etc.) achieve 100\\% accuracy.\nThe only misclassifications arise from \\texttt{GeneratorExit}, a \\texttt{BaseException} subclass that is not an \\texttt{Exception}. It is raised by Python's iterator protocol during generator short-circuiting, not by actual program errors.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Exception type accuracy breakdown for \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Qwen-7B, seed=42). All true error types achieve 100\\%. The only failures come from \\texttt{GeneratorExit} (a \\texttt{BaseException} subclass, not an \\texttt{Exception}), which appears in 2 passing samples.}\n\\label{tab:exception_type}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Exception Type} & \\textbf{Samples} & \\textbf{Accuracy} \\\\\n\\midrule\nTypeError & 96 & 100\\% \\\\\nValueError & 62 & 100\\% \\\\\nIndexError & 41 & 100\\% \\\\\nKeyError & 28 & 100\\% \\\\\nAttributeError & 19 & 100\\% \\\\\nAssertionError & 15 & 100\\% \\\\\nRecursionError & 12 & 100\\% \\\\\nOther Exception & 66 & 100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nGeneratorExit (BaseException) & 2 & 0\\% \\\\\nNo exception (pass class) & 849 & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis confirms that the model has learned a clean decision boundary: any true \\texttt{Exception} subclass in the trace implies failure.\nThe \\texttt{GeneratorExit} case represents a semantic ambiguity in the trace format rather than a model failure, as discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:irreducible}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Trace Length Ablation}\n\\label{sec:trace_length_full}\n\nTo test whether the \\texttt{full\\_trace} performance gap is caused by input truncation at training time, we vary the maximum trace token budget on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42) while keeping all other hyperparameters fixed.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Trace length and truncation direction ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). Head = standard left-truncation (retain first $k$ tokens); Tail = retain last $k$ tokens. Per-condition bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:trace_length}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Direction} & \\textbf{Max Tokens} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nHead & 256  & 78.82 & [76.4, 81.0] \\\\\nHead & 512  & 80.42 & [78.2, 82.6] \\\\\nHead & 1024 & 77.23 & [74.9, 79.6] \\\\\nHead & no\\_limit ($\\infty$) & 81.76 & [79.7, 83.9] \\\\\n\\midrule\nTail & 256  & 77.48 & --- \\\\\nTail & 512  & 93.61 & --- \\\\\nTail & 1024 & 96.47 & --- \\\\\nTail & 2048 & 97.31 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{budget_curve}\n\\caption{Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$, four models, three architecture families). All models remain near baseline at tail-256/384, then jump +13.7--22.0pp at 384$\\to$512, converging into a 93.6--98.2\\% band by tail-1024. The shaded region marks the transition zone where the exception traceback (${\\sim}$400--500 tokens) is captured. Dashed line: \\texttt{exception\\_only} range (99.3--99.9\\%); dotted line: \\texttt{no\\_trace} range (67--77\\%).}\n\\label{fig:trace_length}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAs shown in Table~\\ref{tab:trace_length} and Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_length}, all four trace length conditions fall within a 5pp range (77.2\\%--81.8\\%).\nTo quantify the reliability of the observed differences, Table~\\ref{tab:trace_length_pairwise} reports paired bootstrap comparisons ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42) across all condition pairs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Pairwise paired bootstrap comparisons for trace length ablation ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42). $\\Delta$ = accuracy difference between the two conditions (first $-$ second in the budget group; condition $-$ baseline in the other groups). **$p{<}0.01$; ***$p{<}0.001$.}\n\\label{tab:trace_length_pairwise}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\textbf{Comparison} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$-value} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\rotatebox{90}{\\scriptsize Budget}}\n& 512 vs.\\ 256           & $+$1.60  & [$-$0.34, $+$3.53]   & 0.117 \\\\\n& 1024 vs.\\ 512          & $-$3.19  & [$-$5.29, $-$1.09]   & 0.002** \\\\\n& no\\_limit vs.\\ 1024    & $+$4.54  & [$+$2.52, $+$6.55]   & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n& no\\_limit vs.\\ 256     & $+$2.94  & [$+$0.84, $+$5.04]   & 0.006** \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\rotatebox{90}{\\scriptsize vs.\\ no\\_trace}}\n& 256 $-$ no\\_trace       & $+$3.70  & [$+$1.43, $+$6.05]   & 0.002** \\\\\n& 512 $-$ no\\_trace       & $+$5.29  & [$+$3.03, $+$7.56]   & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n& 1024 $-$ no\\_trace      & $+$2.10  & [$-$0.17, $+$4.37]   & 0.078 \\\\\n& no\\_limit $-$ no\\_trace & $+$6.64  & [$+$4.37, $+$8.99]   & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\rotatebox{90}{\\scriptsize vs.\\ exc.}}\n& 256 $-$ exc\\_only       & $-$21.01 & [$-$23.45, $-$18.74] & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n& 512 $-$ exc\\_only       & $-$19.41 & [$-$21.76, $-$17.14] & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n& 1024 $-$ exc\\_only      & $-$22.61 & [$-$25.04, $-$20.25] & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n& no\\_limit $-$ exc\\_only & $-$18.07 & [$-$20.34, $-$15.88] & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Non-monotonic pattern (head-truncation).}\nUnder head-truncation (retaining trace prefixes), the trend is non-monotonic: 512 tokens (80.42\\%) slightly outperforms 256 tokens (78.82\\%), but doubling to 1024 tokens \\emph{decreases} accuracy to 77.23\\%, a statistically significant 3.19pp drop ($p{=}0.002$, 95\\% CI [$-$5.29, $-$1.09]).\nThe no-limit condition (81.76\\%) recovers and significantly exceeds 1024 tokens ($p{<}0.001$), but 1024 tokens is not significantly better than \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%; $\\Delta{=}{+}2.10$pp, $p{=}0.078$, 95\\% CI [$-$0.17, $+$4.37]).\nThis non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation: the 1024-token prefix retains enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events that appear near the trace terminus.\nUnder tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}); only DeepSeek-6.7B shows a mild $-$1.5pp decrease at tail-1024$\\to$tail-2048, attributable to architecture-specific long-context sensitivity rather than a systematic mid-length pathology.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}\nAll four trace budget conditions significantly exceed \\texttt{no\\_trace} except 1024 tokens ($p{=}0.078$), confirming that raw trace content provides some signal but unreliably so.\nThe gap to \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) is massive for every budget condition (18--23pp, all $p{<}0.001$), underscoring that even unlimited raw traces cannot substitute for targeted signal extraction.\nThe best full-trace condition (no\\_limit, 81.76\\%) still lags \\texttt{exception\\_only} by 18.07pp (95\\% CI [$-$20.34, $-$15.88]).\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThese results rule out input truncation as the primary explanation for the full-trace performance gap: even with unlimited tokens, the model cannot extract the diagnostic signal that VOTA's targeted compression provides.\nThe non-monotonic dip at 1024 tokens is consistent with a ``worst-of-both-worlds'' regime where the model receives enough noisy context to dilute the signal but not enough to compensate through broader coverage.\nThe result reinforces the information density interpretation (\\S\\ref{sec:density}): the gap between full traces and VOTA is driven by the \\emph{content} of the trace (irrelevant lines diluting the diagnostic signal), not its \\emph{length}.\nIn contrast, VOTA's compressed representation concentrates the diagnostic signal into fewer tokens, making it sensitive to truncation when those tokens are removed (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\n\n\\paragraph{Truncation direction.}\nTail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: \\texttt{tail\\_2048} achieves 97.31\\% vs.\\ \\texttt{head\\_2048} (no\\_limit) at 81.76\\%, a 15.55pp improvement.\nEven \\texttt{tail\\_512} (93.61\\%) exceeds every head-truncation condition by over 11pp.\n\nHowever, \\texttt{tail\\_256} (77.48\\%) performs at the \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline level (75.13\\%), indicating that the diagnostic signal does not reside in the very last trace lines.\nThe critical jump occurs between 256 and 512 tail tokens (+16.13pp), localizing the signal window to approximately 256--512 tokens from the trace terminus.\nThis is consistent with Python's execution model: exception tracebacks and assertion comparisons appear near the end of the trace but are preceded by the final function call frames, placing them typically 300--500 tokens from the terminus rather than at the absolute end.\n\nThe 18.07pp gap from \\texttt{full\\_trace} to \\texttt{exception\\_only} decomposes as: 86\\% positional concentration (recovered by tail-truncation at 2048 tokens) and 14\\% residual content noise (the 2.52pp gap from \\texttt{tail\\_2048} to \\texttt{exception\\_only} that persists regardless of truncation direction).\nThe fact that \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) still outperforms \\texttt{tail\\_2048} (97.31\\%) confirms that semantic extraction (identifying the exception type and message) is superior to positional extraction (keeping a fixed window), because the model need not filter out non-diagnostic lines that happen to fall within the window.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model budget curve.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} extends the tail-truncation analysis to a full budget sweep across four models spanning three architecture families and an 8.7$\\times$ parameter range.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$). All four models exhibit a transition between tail-384 and tail-512 (+13--23pp). $\\downarrow$: non-monotonic decrease. $^\\dag$CodeLlama tail-1024 trained with label-only loss to address tokenizer-driven gradient dilution (\\S\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}). $^\\ddag$CodeLlama tail-2048 outlier (41.8\\%): LoRA long-sequence instability under label-only loss; excluded from cross-model analysis.}\n\\label{tab:tail_cross_model}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{tail-256} & \\textbf{tail-384} & \\textbf{tail-512} & \\textbf{tail-1024} & \\textbf{tail-2048} & \\textbf{exc\\_only} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-7B (7B) & 78.9 & 79.9 & 93.6 & 96.5 & 97.3 & 99.83 \\\\\nDeepSeek (6.7B) & 76.4 & 82.9 & 93.9 & 96.5 & 95.0$\\downarrow$ & 99.33 \\\\\nCodeLlama (13B) & 77.9 & 80.6 & 98.2 & 98.2$^\\dag$ & 41.8$^\\ddag$ & 99.89 \\\\\nQwen-1.5B (1.5B) & 72.6 & 73.6 & 95.6 & 96.6 & 97.9 & 99.83 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model truncation direction comparison ($N{=}1{,}190$). Head-truncation retains the first 2,048 tokens; tail-truncation retains the last 2,048. All four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 signal-poor band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp). $^\\dag$CodeLlama tail-2048 pending retraining (\\S\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:head_vs_tail}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{head-2048 (\\%)} & \\textbf{tail-2048 (\\%)} & \\textbf{Gap (pp)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B & 75.3 & 97.9 & +22.6 \\\\\nDeepSeek-6.7B & 78.7 & 95.0 & +16.3 \\\\\nCodeLlama-13B & 79.9 & ---$^\\dag$ & ---$^\\dag$ \\\\\nQwen-7B & 81.8 & 97.3 & +15.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nFour patterns emerge from the budget curve.\n\\emph{Phase transition between 384 and 512 tokens}: the tail-384 column reveals that the transition is not instantaneous at 256$\\to$512 but localized between 384 and 512 tokens.\nAt tail-384, all four models remain near baseline (73.6--82.9\\%), gaining only +1.0--6.5pp over tail-256.\nThe steep jump occurs at 384$\\to$512: +13.7pp (Qwen-7B), +11.0pp (DeepSeek), +17.6pp (CodeLlama), +22.0pp (Qwen-1.5B), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling.\nThis localizes the critical threshold to ${\\sim}$400--500 tokens, consistent with the typical exception traceback length: below ${\\sim}$384 tokens, the traceback is truncated and no diagnostic event is captured; above ${\\sim}$512, it is fully included regardless of model capacity or architecture.\n\n\\emph{Convergence at tail-512+}: once the phase transition is crossed, accuracy converges across all models into a 93.6--98.2\\% band at tail-512 (seed 42).\nMulti-seed validation confirms robustness: across all 12 model$\\times$seed combinations at tail-512 (Table~\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}), all accuracies fall within [93.61\\%, 98.24\\%], a 4.63pp band that compresses the signal-poor tail-256 spread (6.3pp) by 27\\%.\nCodeLlama-13B shows perfect per-model convergence at tail-512: all three seeds achieve 98.24\\% ($\\sigma{=}$0), consistent with the 13B scale and label-only loss providing a stronger optimization signal in the signal-rich regime (Table~\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-seed convergence at the tail-512 truncation point ($N{=}1{,}190$ per seed). All 12 model$\\times$seed combinations fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\\%, 98.24\\%]. CodeLlama-13B achieves identical accuracy across all three seeds ($\\sigma{=}$0).}\n\\label{tab:tail512_seeds}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{S42} & \\textbf{S123} & \\textbf{S456} & \\textbf{Mean} & \\textbf{$\\sigma$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-7B (7B)      & 93.61 & 96.00 & 97.65 & 95.75 & 2.05 \\\\\nDeepSeek (6.7B)    & 93.95 & 95.04 & 96.10 & 95.03 & 1.08 \\\\\nQwen-1.5B (1.5B)  & 95.63 & 97.65 & 96.72 & 96.67 & 1.01 \\\\\nCodeLlama (13B)    & 98.24 & 98.24 & 98.24 & \\textbf{98.24} & \\textbf{0.00} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{Cross-model band} & \\multicolumn{2}{r}{[93.61, 98.24] = 4.63pp} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt tail-1024, four models reach 96.5--98.2\\% (CodeLlama 98.2\\% under label-only loss, three others 96.5--96.6\\%).\nScale and architecture differences that dominate the signal-poor regime (tail-256 range: 72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp) compress dramatically in the signal-rich regime.\n\n\\emph{Signal-poor convergence band}: head-2048 accuracies across all four models also converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp; Table~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}), comparable in width to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp).\nWhen the diagnostic signal is absent (head-truncation discards trace terminus) or unreachable (tail-256 is too short), models of different scale and architecture collapse to similar performance, suggesting a shared floor determined by code comprehension rather than trace processing.\n\n\\emph{Scale behavior inversion}: in the signal-poor regime (tail-256), smaller models perform worse (Qwen-1.5B 72.6\\% vs.\\ Qwen-7B 78.9\\%, $-$6.3pp), mirroring the \\texttt{no\\_trace} scale pattern.\nIn the signal-rich regime (tail-512+), this reverses: Qwen-1.5B matches or exceeds Qwen-7B (95.6\\% vs.\\ 93.6\\% at tail-512; 97.9\\% vs.\\ 97.3\\% at tail-2048), confirming that sufficient trace signal eliminates the capacity advantage.\n\n\\emph{Non-monotonicity at long sequences}: two models show accuracy decreases beyond tail-1024.\nDeepSeek-6.7B drops $-$1.5pp from tail-1024 (96.5\\%) to tail-2048 (95.0\\%), consistent with its known long-context sensitivity (\\S\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\nCodeLlama-13B exhibits a more severe collapse: tail-2048 under label-only loss drops to 41.8\\% (vs.\\ tail-1024 = 98.2\\%), which we attribute to LoRA long-sequence instability under the label-only loss regime; this outlier is excluded from cross-model tail-2048 analysis.\nQwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B increase monotonically across all budgets.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail} confirms the truncation direction effect: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 outperforms head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp.\nThe gap is largest for Qwen-1.5B (+22.6pp), indicating that smaller models benefit most from positional proximity to the diagnostic signal, as they lack the capacity to locate it within longer, noisier prefixes.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Calibration Analysis}\n\\label{sec:calibration_full}\n\nBeyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \\citep{spiess2025calibration}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{ECE $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{Brier $\\downarrow$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & \\textbf{0.0004} & \\textbf{0.0008} \\\\\nexception\\_only & 0.0027 & 0.0013 \\\\\nVOTA & 0.0054 & 0.005 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 0.096 & 0.144 \\\\\nno\\_trace & 0.171 & 0.197 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.\nTier~1 conditions (\\texttt{ae\\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \\texttt{exception\\_only}: ECE=0.0027) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \\texttt{no\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.\nVOTA (ECE=0.0054) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.\n\nThe Brier scores tell a consistent story: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (0.0008) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \\texttt{no\\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model calibration.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration_cross} extends the calibration analysis to three models under \\texttt{exception\\_only}.\nAll three models achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 and Brier ${<}$ 0.007, confirming that calibration quality generalizes across architectures.\nCodeLlama-13B achieves the lowest ECE (0.0020) and MCE (0.0411), while DeepSeek-6.7B shows the highest ECE (0.0064) and Brier (0.0061), consistent with its higher error rate (8 errors vs.\\ 2 for Qwen/CodeLlama).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model calibration for \\texttt{exception\\_only} ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score; MCE = maximum calibration error. All models achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007.}\n\\label{tab:calibration_cross}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{ECE $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{Brier $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{MCE $\\downarrow$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nCodeLlama-13B & \\textbf{0.0020} & \\textbf{0.0013} & \\textbf{0.0411} \\\\\nQwen-7B & 0.0027 & \\textbf{0.0013} & 0.8355 \\\\\nDeepSeek-6.7B & 0.0064 & 0.0061 & 0.7756 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_calibration}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams for four trace conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). \\texttt{exception\\_only} (ECE=0.0027) clusters near the diagonal, while \\texttt{no\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibits systematic overconfidence, a 63$\\times$ gap.}\n\\label{fig:calibration}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Error Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{sec:error_overlap}\n\nWe analyze the overlap between error sets of different conditions to determine whether they fail on the same samples (Figure~\\ref{fig:error_overlap}).\n\n\\texttt{exception\\_only}'s 2 errors (samples 901 and 905) have zero intersection with the error sets of all other conditions (Jaccard = 0.000 in all pairwise comparisons).\nThis orthogonality arises because \\texttt{exception\\_only} errors are caused by \\texttt{GeneratorExit} false positives (pass samples misclassified as fail due to benign \\texttt{BaseException} events), while errors from other conditions are predominantly fail samples misclassified as pass due to insufficient signal.\n\nAmong non-exception conditions, error sets exhibit hierarchical containment: \\texttt{ae\\_only} $\\subset$ VOTA $\\subset$ \\texttt{loop\\_only} $\\subset$ \\texttt{no\\_trace}, with pairwise Jaccard indices increasing with signal overlap.\n\\texttt{loop\\_only} and \\texttt{full\\_trace} share the highest overlap (Jaccard ${\\sim}$0.65), reflecting their shared reliance on behavioral patterns rather than explicit fault indicators.\n\nThis analysis confirms that \\texttt{exception\\_only} operates on an independent signal dimension: its errors are representation ambiguities (iterator protocol artifacts) rather than information deficits, explaining why combining it with other signals cannot eliminate these specific errors.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{error_overlap}\n\\caption{Jaccard similarity between error sets of different conditions (Qwen-7B, seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$). \\texttt{exception\\_only} (exc\\_only) has zero overlap with all other conditions, confirming it operates on an independent signal dimension.}\n\\label{fig:error_overlap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Cross-Dataset Generalization: DebugBench}\n\\label{sec:debugbench_full}\n\nTo test whether exception-based verdict prediction generalizes beyond LLM-generated solutions, we evaluate on DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench}, a dataset of human-written programs with synthetically injected bugs (single-point mutations such as operator replacement, variable misuse, and missing statements).\nAfter filtering for executable Python samples with valid traces, we retain 426 samples with a near-balanced class distribution (majority baseline: 51.4\\%).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Generalization to DebugBench ($N{=}426$, Qwen-2.5-7B, seed=42). Wilson 95\\% CIs. The component ranking (exception\\_only $\\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\\_trace) is preserved, and exception\\_only achieves perfect accuracy.}\n\\label{tab:debugbench}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{100.00} & [99.14, 100.00] \\\\\nVOTA & 99.77 & [99.30, 100.00] \\\\\nno\\_trace & 96.71 & [94.80, 98.40] \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & 51.41 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:debugbench} shows that the component ranking from the main experiments is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (100.00\\%) $\\geq$ VOTA (99.77\\%) $>$ \\texttt{no\\_trace} (96.71\\%).\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition achieves perfect accuracy, confirming that the exception-based representation generalizes to a different fault distribution without degradation.\n\nThe absolute margins are much narrower than on the main dataset: the \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline already achieves 96.71\\% (compared to 75.13\\% on HumanEval+MBPP), leaving only 3.29pp of headroom for trace-based improvement.\nThis ceiling effect reflects a fundamental difference in fault characteristics: DebugBench bugs are synthetically injected single-point mutations, which produce code that is structurally dissimilar from the original correct solution; a model can often identify the fault from source code alone.\nIn contrast, the HumanEval+MBPP solutions are LLM-generated, producing natural errors (logic mistakes, edge-case failures) where the faulty and correct solutions may be structurally similar, making code-only verdict prediction substantially harder.\n\nThese results support exception-based verdict prediction as a generalizable approach: the method does not degrade on an out-of-distribution dataset, and the exception dominance ranking (exception\\_only $\\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\\_trace) is consistent across both datasets. However, the narrow DebugBench margins are insufficient to independently validate trace importance.\nThe 24.70pp trace lift on the main dataset (no\\_trace 75.13\\% $\\to$ exception\\_only 99.83\\%) remains the primary evidence for the value of exception-based trace signals.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Cross-Model Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{sec:robustness_analysis}\n\nThe cross-model gap pattern (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \\emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}\nException events are consistently the most effective component on both models, and their structural simplicity (a fixed-format error message) ensures that the relative advantage over richer representations holds across all seeds.\nTwo of three DeepSeek seeds match Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\\%); the outlier (seed 123, 91.09\\%) we hypothesize reflects LoRA initialization sensitivity landing in a suboptimal basin (fail recall = 70.5\\%, though direct evidence is lacking), not a failure of the exception signal itself; even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp.\nThe VOTA cross-model gap is confounded by two factors: (1)~tokenizer efficiency (the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus Qwen's 1{,}929, causing 91.7\\% of training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window; content-aware truncation preferentially removes the terminal structured prompt, preserving the verdict label but truncating trace content) and (2)~base model capability differences, consistent with findings that SFT generalization depends on pretrain quality \\citep{yang2024generalization}.\nThe two factors cannot be disentangled in the current experimental design.\nOur \\texttt{exception\\_only} design sidesteps the truncation confound entirely: inputs have a median of 150 tokens (mean 398), well below the 2{,}048-token context window, so no training samples are truncated on either model.\nThe resulting 0.56pp cross-model gap (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) is therefore a clean measure of model capability differences, making the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.\nThe practical implication is that trace-based systems should default to the simplest sufficient signal; for smaller models, training stability (multi-seed selection, warmup schedules) is a secondary but addressable concern.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Training Dynamics and Loss Decoupling}\n\\label{sec:training_dynamics}\n\nThe DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} results reveal two instructive training dynamics.\nFirst, epoch-level analysis shows mild degradation in 2/3 seeds: seed 42 peaks at 99.83\\% after epoch~1 but settles at 99.33\\% by epoch~3 ($-$0.50pp), seed 123 degrades from 91.43\\% to 91.09\\% ($-$0.34pp), while seed 456 shows no epoch degradation (99.33\\% at both epoch~1 and epoch~3).\nEarly stopping may yield marginal gains for some seeds, but the epoch effect is small (${\\leq}$0.5pp) compared to the seed effect (8.24pp between seeds 42 and 123).\n\nSecond, seeds 123 and 456 converge to nearly identical final training losses (0.122 and 0.123 respectively), yet their accuracies differ by 8.24pp (91.09\\% vs.\\ 99.33\\%).\nThe critical difference lies in fail recall: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 100\\%, while seed 123 achieves only 70.5\\%. The LoRA initialization in seed 123 fails to learn the exception$\\to$fail mapping for a substantial fraction of failing samples, even though the per-token loss appears converged.\nThis demonstrates that token-level training loss can mask fundamental differences in sample-level classification ability, suggesting a parallel to the eval-loss/accuracy decoupling observed in DeepSeek VOTA (where eval loss rose 65\\% from epoch 1 to 3 while accuracy improved by 6.3pp) at the training phase.\nThe practical recommendation is twofold: (1)~use task-specific metrics (accuracy, fail recall) for checkpoint and seed selection rather than training or evaluation loss, and (2)~for deployment on smaller models, train multiple seeds and select the best, as a single run may not reliably converge to the correct classification boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Format collapse, not semantic failure.}\nThe 100 errors of seed 123 are not semantic failures but generation format collapse: on inputs exceeding 4,000 characters (those containing verbose locals dumps), the model echoes trace content instead of emitting a verdict token, while achieving zero errors on shorter inputs.\nThis indicates that the LoRA initialization of seed 123 specifically compromises long-context format compliance rather than exception signal comprehension, further supporting the robustness advantage of compact \\texttt{exception\\_only} inputs.\n\nThe contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\\% to 84.45\\% across epochs, but is consistent with a performance limit associated with the high trace truncation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}): the model progressively extracts signal from samples with intact trace content but cannot fully compensate for the information loss in the truncated majority.\nThe 84.45\\% accuracy reflects a combination of trace information loss and model capability constraints.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Deduplication Statistics}\n\\label{sec:dedup_stats}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dedup} reports the duplication rates across trace formats.\nBecause multiple passing solutions to the same problem produce identical (input, output) pairs in the \\texttt{exception\\_only} format (correct executions raise no exceptions, so the trace content is the same regardless of the solution's internal logic), the dataset contains duplicate rows.\nTrain--test input overlap is zero (split by problem ID), so no information leakage occurs.\nThe high duplication in \\texttt{exception\\_only} does not affect our experimental conclusions: after deduplication the training set still contains 2,858 unique samples and the test set 607, providing sufficient coverage for the 591-problem benchmark.\n\n\\paragraph{Problem-level partitioning and sensitivity analysis.}\nData is partitioned at the problem level: 472 training problems and 119 test problems, with zero crossover.\nDuplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem (up to 10 solutions each), not from cross-problem overlap.\nTo verify that deduplication does not alter any finding, we ran a sensitivity analysis across all 19 experimental conditions (7 trace formats $\\times$ 4 models, minus conditions not run): deduplication produces a 0.00pp accuracy delta in every case.\nThis confirms that duplicate samples neither inflate nor deflate accuracy, because duplicates share the same ground-truth label and the model's prediction is deterministic at inference time.\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} test set contains 48.99\\% duplicate inputs due to representation compactness; all confidence intervals treat samples as independent Bernoulli trials.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Deduplication statistics. The \\texttt{exception\\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows because correct executions produce no exception events. Train--test input overlap is 0 (no leakage).}\n\\label{tab:dedup}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Split} & \\textbf{Total} & \\textbf{Unique} & \\textbf{Dup.\\ rate} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only train & 4{,}720 & 2{,}858 & 39.45\\% \\\\\nexception\\_only test  & 1{,}190 &   607 & 48.99\\% \\\\\nraw\\_traces (full)    & 5{,}910 & 5{,}803 &  1.81\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Regex Baseline Analysis}\n\\label{sec:regex_baselines}\n\nTo establish a non-learning upper bound, we evaluate six regex-based baselines on the \\texttt{exception\\_only} test set ($N{=}1{,}190$) and two format-specific baselines on VOTA and \\texttt{full\\_trace} inputs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Regex baseline accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). B4 is the strongest semantic baseline (6.22pp below SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only}, McNemar $p{<}0.001$). B6 uses an error count threshold. $^\\ddag$B5 exploits a data formatting artifact: the \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker is deterministically present in all pass samples, making this a format-level shortcut rather than a genuine exception-based heuristic.}\n\\label{tab:regex}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Baseline} & \\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nB1: \\texttt{[ERROR]} presence & string match & 93.45 \\\\\nB2: Traceback presence & string match & 71.51 \\\\\nB3: Any keyword & union of B1+B2+Exception & 93.45 \\\\\nB4: Exception type regex & regex on exception class names & \\textbf{93.61} \\\\\nB5: Format marker & \\texttt{[No exception data]} $\\to$ pass & 99.33$^\\ddag$ \\\\\nB6: Error count $\\geq$2 & count threshold & 92.02 \\\\\n\\midrule\nVOTA format regex & best regex on VOTA traces & 75.55 \\\\\nfull\\_trace format regex & best regex on full traces & 77.39 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe strongest legitimate baseline (B4, 93.61\\%) applies regex pattern-matching on Python exception class names (e.g., \\texttt{ValueError}, \\texttt{TypeError}).\nIts errors concentrate on 8 boundary samples where a \\texttt{ValueError} is raised inside a \\texttt{try/except} block and caught; the program passes despite an exception occurring in the trace.\nSFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} correctly classifies 6 of these 8 samples, indicating that the model learns the semantic distinction between caught (benign) and uncaught (fault-indicating) exceptions.\nB5 (99.33\\%) achieves near-SFT accuracy but relies on the deterministic presence of a format marker in pass-class traces, which is an artifact of the trace preprocessing pipeline rather than a generalizable signal; McNemar test vs.\\ SFT yields $p{=}0.041$, confirming a statistically significant difference.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-format comparison.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:regex_cross_format} compares SFT and the best applicable regex baseline for each trace format.\nThe SFT advantage increases with format complexity: \\texttt{no\\_trace} (+3.62pp over majority baseline) $<$ \\texttt{full\\_trace} (+4.37pp) $<$ \\texttt{exception\\_only} (+6.22pp) $\\ll$ VOTA (+23.86pp).\nThe VOTA gap is largest because its structured multi-component format carries semantic content that regex cannot parse, while \\texttt{exception\\_only}'s simpler format allows regex to capture most of the signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{SFT vs.\\ best regex baseline by trace format ($N{=}1{,}190$). Regex baselines: majority class for \\texttt{no\\_trace}, keyword matching for \\texttt{full\\_trace}, pattern matching for VOTA, exception type regex (B4) for \\texttt{exception\\_only}.}\n\\label{tab:regex_cross_format}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Format} & \\textbf{SFT (\\%)} & \\textbf{Regex (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & 99.83 & 93.61 & +6.22 \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05 & 75.55 & +23.50 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & 77.39 & +4.37 \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13 & 71.51 & +3.62 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Cross-Model Zero-Shot Comparison}\n\\label{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:zeroshot_cross_model} compares zero-shot and SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} accuracy across four models.\nZero-shot performance does not correlate with model size: the smallest model (Qwen-1.5B, 93.45\\%) outperforms all larger models, while the largest (CodeLlama-13B) falls below majority baseline.\nSFT compresses the 42.44pp zero-shot range to 0.56pp, eliminating architecture-dependent variance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Zero-shot vs.\\ SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} across four models ($N{=}1{,}190$). Zero-shot accuracy is uncorrelated with model size; SFT normalizes cross-architecture variance from 42.44pp to 0.56pp.}\n\\label{tab:zeroshot_cross_model}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Size} & \\textbf{Zero-shot (\\%)} & \\textbf{SFT (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B & 1.5B & 93.45 & 99.83 & +6.38 \\\\\nDeepSeek-6.7B & 6.7B & 90.59 & 99.33 & +8.74 \\\\\nQwen-7B & 7B & 89.08 & 99.83 & +10.75 \\\\\nCodeLlama-13B & 13B & 51.01 & 99.89 & +48.88 \\\\\n\\midrule\nRange & --- & 42.44pp & 0.56pp & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n",

```text
1	\documentclass[11pt,a4paper]{article}
2	\usepackage[review]{acl}
3	% \usepackage{acl}  % uncomment for camera-ready (remove [review])
4	\usepackage{times,latexsym}
5	\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb}

8	\usepackage{booktabs}
9	\usepackage{multirow}
10	\usepackage{xcolor}
11	\usepackage{algorithm}
12	\usepackage{algorithmic}
13	
14	\title{Less Is More: Why Exception Events Outperform Complete Traces \\for Verdict Prediction}
15	\author{Anonymous ACL submission}
16	
17	\begin{document}
18	\maketitle
19	
20	\begin{abstract}
21	Code language models increasingly incorporate execution traces, yet more trace information does not guarantee better performance.
22	We present the first systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction---classifying whether a test passes or fails---across four models spanning three architecture families and a 1.5B--13B parameter range.
23	Exception events alone achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% accuracy on three of four models, while complete traces plateau roughly 18 percentage points lower despite containing strictly more information.
24	Training dynamics reveal why: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 training steps, whereas \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates throughout training without reaching stable accuracy---overfitting to spurious patterns in verbose content rather than learning the verdict signal.
25	Decomposition analysis attributes the gap to information overload and gradient dilution from low-density tokens, and a tail-truncation budget sweep confirms a sharp phase transition between compact and verbose inputs.
26	These results demonstrate that for trace-based verdict prediction, information density determines learning efficiency far more than information quantity.
27	\end{abstract}
28	
29	\input{introduction}
30	\input{method}
31	\input{experiments}
32	\input{discussion}
33	\input{related_work}
34	\input{conclusion}
35	
36	\section*{Limitations}
37	
38	Our study has several limitations.
39	First, our primary experiments use a single dataset (591 problems from HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized); while a cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench (\S\ref{sec:debugbench}) confirms that exception dominance generalizes to synthetically injected bugs, generalization to other languages, larger codebases, or real-world fault distributions remains untested.
40	Second, while we evaluate four models spanning 1.5B to 13B parameters across three architecture families, all are code-specialized; generalization to general-purpose models or larger scales remains untested.
41	Third, DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\% ($\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), which we hypothesize reflects LoRA initialization sensitivity in DeepSeek-Coder, though direct evidence is lacking (fail recall = 70.5\% for the outlier vs.\ 100\% for others).
42	Qwen \texttt{exception\_only} shows perfect seed robustness ($\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$), but Qwen \texttt{ae\_only} also exhibits seed variance ($\sigma{=}$0.40pp; seed 123 = 99.08\% vs.\ seeds 42/456 = 99.92\%), though far smaller than DeepSeek's ($\sigma{=}$4.76pp).
43	Whether training stabilization techniques can eliminate DeepSeek's seed sensitivity is unexplored; other conditions use a single seed.
44	Fourth, the initial DeepSeek \texttt{full\_trace} run was invalidated by context overflow; a re-run with extended context yields 79.16\% (CI [76.80\%, 81.30\%]), 2.60pp below Qwen (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
45	Fifth, the candidate solutions were generated by Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct, which also serves as one of our evaluation models; this does not affect verdict prediction but may influence the pass/fail distribution.
46	Sixth, self-familiarity bias may inflate absolute accuracy: both Qwen-2.5-Coder and DeepSeek-Coder were likely exposed to HumanEval and MBPP solutions during pretraining, so the models may partially recognize solutions rather than purely reasoning from trace signals; our cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench mitigates but does not eliminate this concern.
47	Seventh, the error distribution in our test set may not reflect real-world fault distributions: exceptions (especially \texttt{TypeError}, \texttt{ValueError}) dominate LLM-generated failures, but production code may exhibit a broader spectrum of silent logic errors where no exception is raised, potentially reducing the effectiveness of exception-based verdict prediction.
48	Finally, our analysis is limited to verdict prediction; whether the information density advantage extends to other trace-based tasks is an open question.
49	
50	\section*{Ethics Statement}
51	
52	This work uses publicly available benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP-sanitized, DebugBench) and does not involve human subjects, crowdsourced annotations, or personally identifiable information.
53	All models evaluated are open-source and publicly accessible.
54	Training uses parameter-efficient LoRA fine-tuning on two RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPUs, with each experiment using a single GPU.
55	Total compute is approximately 320 GPU-hours across all conditions, models, seeds, and iterative refinements (including approximately 170 hours for successful experiments; individual runs range from 0.5h for small models to 2.2h for CodeLlama-13B, with the total reflecting 4 models $\times$ multiple conditions $\times$ multiple seeds $\times$ iterative development).
56	We do not foresee direct negative societal impacts from this work; verdict prediction is a developer-facing tool for automated testing.
57	
58	\bibliography{references}
```

```text
docs/paper/experiments.tex:60:The CodeLlama result (17 errors) narrows the gap: among models without tokenizer-driven truncation, the VOTA range is only 1.01pp (98.57--99.58\%), suggesting that the 14.96pp DeepSeek deficit is primarily a truncation artifact rather than an inherent model capability limitation (91.7\% of DeepSeek training samples were affected; Appendix~\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).

docs/paper/appendix.tex:253:The VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}; confounded by tokenizer-driven truncation; 91.7\% of DeepSeek training samples were affected) is driven by a training-time truncation asymmetry.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:254:The DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}929 for Qwen, causing 91.7\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:257:A post-hoc group analysis provides directional evidence: partitioning DeepSeek VOTA test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\% (95\% CI [80.75\%, 85.24\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class (fail recall = 31.23\%).
docs/paper/appendix.tex:258:Per-class analysis (Table~\ref{tab:per_class}) confirms that DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \texttt{pass}, in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\% fail recall.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:404:These results rule out input truncation as the primary explanation for the full-trace performance gap: even with unlimited tokens, the model cannot extract the diagnostic signal that VOTA's targeted compression provides.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:406:The result reinforces the information density interpretation (\S\ref{sec:density}): the gap between full traces and VOTA is driven by the \emph{content} of the trace (irrelevant lines diluting the diagnostic signal), not its \emph{length}.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:407:In contrast, VOTA's compressed representation concentrates the diagnostic signal into fewer tokens, making it sensitive to truncation when those tokens are removed (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
docs/paper/appendix.tex:526:VOTA & 0.0054 & 0.005 \\
docs/paper/appendix.tex:535:VOTA (ECE=0.0054) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:576:Among non-exception conditions, error sets exhibit hierarchical containment: \texttt{ae\_only} $\subset$ VOTA $\subset$ \texttt{loop\_only} $\subset$ \texttt{no\_trace}, with pairwise Jaccard indices increasing with signal overlap.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:597:\caption{Generalization to DebugBench ($N{=}426$, Qwen-2.5-7B, seed=42). Wilson 95\% CIs. The component ranking (exception\_only $\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\_trace) is preserved, and exception\_only achieves perfect accuracy.}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:605:VOTA & 99.77 & [99.30, 100.00] \\
docs/paper/appendix.tex:613:Table~\ref{tab:debugbench} shows that the component ranking from the main experiments is preserved: \texttt{exception\_only} (100.00\%) $\geq$ VOTA (99.77\%) $>$ \texttt{no\_trace} (96.71\%).
docs/paper/appendix.tex:620:These results support exception-based verdict prediction as a generalizable approach: the method does not degrade on an out-of-distribution dataset, and the exception dominance ranking (exception\_only $\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\_trace) is consistent across both datasets. However, the narrow DebugBench margins are insufficient to independently validate trace importance.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:629:Two of three DeepSeek seeds match Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\%); the outlier (seed 123, 91.09\%) we hypothesize reflects LoRA initialization sensitivity landing in a suboptimal basin (fail recall = 70.5\%, though direct evidence is lacking), not a failure of the exception signal itself; even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\%) by 6.64pp.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:630:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/appendix.tex:646:This demonstrates that token-level training loss can mask fundamental differences in sample-level classification ability, suggesting a parallel to the eval-loss/accuracy decoupling observed in DeepSeek VOTA (where eval loss rose 65\% from epoch 1 to 3 while accuracy improved by 6.3pp) at the training phase.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:653:The contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\% to 84.45\% across epochs, but is consistent with a performance limit associated with the high trace truncation rate (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}): the model progressively extracts signal from samples with intact trace content but cannot fully compensate for the information loss in the truncated majority.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:692:To establish a non-learning upper bound, we evaluate six regex-based baselines on the \texttt{exception\_only} test set ($N{=}1{,}190$) and two format-specific baselines on VOTA and \texttt{full\_trace} inputs.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:711:VOTA format regex & best regex on VOTA traces & 75.55 \\
docs/paper/appendix.tex:725:The SFT advantage increases with format complexity: \texttt{no\_trace} (+3.62pp over majority baseline) $<$ \texttt{full\_trace} (+4.37pp) $<$ \texttt{exception\_only} (+6.22pp) $\ll$ VOTA (+23.86pp).
docs/paper/appendix.tex:726:The VOTA gap is largest because its structured multi-component format carries semantic content that regex cannot parse, while \texttt{exception\_only}'s simpler format allows regex to capture most of the signal.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:730:\caption{SFT vs.\ best regex baseline by trace format ($N{=}1{,}190$). Regex baselines: majority class for \texttt{no\_trace}, keyword matching for \texttt{full\_trace}, pattern matching for VOTA, exception type regex (B4) for \texttt{exception\_only}.}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:738:VOTA & 99.05 & 75.55 & +23.50 \\
docs/paper/introduction.tex:12:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/introduction.tex:16:DeepSeek-6.7B is the exception: its 3-seed mean is 96.58\% ($\pm$4.76pp) due to one seed exhibiting format collapse (fail recall = 70.5\%), though even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\%) by 6.64pp.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:20:Adding trace information beyond exceptions provides no benefit and can degrade performance: VOTA (3-seed mean 99.05\%) underperforms \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) on Qwen-7B, with the gap reaching 14.96pp on DeepSeek due to tokenizer-driven truncation.
docs/paper/arr_checklist.tex:72:\item \textbf{Data}: Execution traces, VOTA-compressed traces, and train/test splits will be released. Raw traces are generated deterministically from publicly available benchmarks using Python \texttt{sys.settrace}.
```

</details>

```text
docs/paper/related_work.tex:29:Neural network calibration \citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration,spiess2025calibration}, studies confidence-correctness alignment; our ECE ${<}$ 0.007 suggests compact inputs improve calibration.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:169:\label{sec:calibration}
docs/paper/experiments.tex:171:This calibration advantage generalizes across models: all three tested architectures achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 under \texttt{exception\_only} (Appendix~\ref{sec:calibration_full}).
docs/paper/discussion.tex:17:Zero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\% (42.44pp range), which SFT compresses to 0.56pp; three-shot ICL (90.84\%) improves marginally, confirming the bottleneck is verdict calibration rather than exception recognition.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:511:\label{sec:calibration_full}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:513:Beyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \citep{spiess2025calibration}.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:517:\caption{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:518:\label{tab:calibration}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:533:Table~\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:534:Tier~1 conditions (\texttt{ae\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \texttt{exception\_only}: ECE=0.0027) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \texttt{full\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \texttt{no\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:537:The Brier scores tell a consistent story: \texttt{ae\_only} (0.0008) and \texttt{exception\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \texttt{no\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:539:\paragraph{Cross-model calibration.}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:540:Table~\ref{tab:calibration_cross} extends the calibration analysis to three models under \texttt{exception\_only}.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:541:All three models achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 and Brier ${<}$ 0.007, confirming that calibration quality generalizes across architectures.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:546:\caption{Cross-model calibration for \texttt{exception\_only} ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score; MCE = maximum calibration error. All models achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007.}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:547:\label{tab:calibration_cross}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:562:\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig2_calibration}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:564:\label{fig:calibration}

docs/paper/references.bib:370:@inproceedings{guo2017calibration,
```

```text
docs/paper/experiments.tex:44:Excluding the DeepSeek outlier, the cross-model range is 0.56pp (99.33--99.89\%), the tightest of any condition.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:61:In contrast, \texttt{exception\_only} inputs are compact (median 150 tokens, mean 398 due to verbose fail-class exceptions), making the cross-model comparison clean.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:66:We restrict \texttt{ae\_only} and \texttt{loop\_only} ablations to Qwen-7B, as these component-level analyses target the trace composition mechanism rather than cross-model generalization.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:67:Table~\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B and reveals a two-tier hierarchy on this model; the cross-model consistency of \texttt{exception\_only} (${\geq}$99.33\% on all four models) suggests Tier~1 generalizes, though direct Tier~2 verification on other models remains future work.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:147:This analysis uses 3 seeds on a single model (Qwen-7B); cross-model validation remains future work.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:161:A cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a phase transition localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:164:Beyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\times$seed combinations confirms all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\%, 98.24\%] (Table~\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.
docs/paper/main.tex:42:Qwen \texttt{exception\_only} shows perfect seed robustness ($\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$), but Qwen \texttt{ae\_only} also exhibits seed variance ($\sigma{=}$0.40pp; seed 123 = 99.08\% vs.\ seeds 42/456 = 99.92\%), though far smaller than DeepSeek's ($\sigma{=}$4.76pp).
docs/paper/discussion.tex:7:The 0.56pp cross-model gap supports that signal density, not model capacity, is the binding constraint.
docs/paper/method.tex:41:The primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \texttt{exception\_only} robustness (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_details}).
docs/paper/appendix.tex:253:The VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}; confounded by tokenizer-driven truncation; 91.7\% of DeepSeek training samples were affected) is driven by a training-time truncation asymmetry.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:260:Seed robustness diverges across models: Qwen \texttt{exception\_only} produces identical 99.83\% across all three seeds ($\sigma{=}0$), while DeepSeek shows bimodal behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\% (fail recall = 100\%), seed 123 yields 91.09\% (fail recall = 70.5\%).
docs/paper/appendix.tex:425:\caption{Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$). All four models exhibit a transition between tail-384 and tail-512 (+13--23pp). $\downarrow$: non-monotonic decrease. $^\dag$CodeLlama tail-1024 trained with label-only loss to address tokenizer-driven gradient dilution (\S\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}). $^\ddag$CodeLlama tail-2048 outlier (41.8\%): LoRA long-sequence instability under label-only loss; excluded from cross-model analysis.}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:468:Multi-seed validation confirms robustness: across all 12 model$\times$seed combinations at tail-512 (Table~\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}), all accuracies fall within [93.61\%, 98.24\%], a 4.63pp band that compresses the signal-poor tail-256 spread (6.3pp) by 27\%.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:503:CodeLlama-13B exhibits a more severe collapse: tail-2048 under label-only loss drops to 41.8\% (vs.\ tail-1024 = 98.2\%), which we attribute to LoRA long-sequence instability under the label-only loss regime; this outlier is excluded from cross-model tail-2048 analysis.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:625:\label{sec:robustness_analysis}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:627:The cross-model gap pattern (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:630:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/appendix.tex:633:The resulting 0.56pp cross-model gap (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) is therefore a clean measure of model capability differences, making the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:651:This indicates that the LoRA initialization of seed 123 specifically compromises long-context format compliance rather than exception signal comprehension, further supporting the robustness advantage of compact \texttt{exception\_only} inputs.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:30:\item \textbf{Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy (Qwen-7B; cross-model verification in progress)}: Tier~1 signals (exceptions, assertions) individually achieve ${\geq}$99\%; Tier~2 (loop compression) reaches 86.1\%, still +10.9pp over no trace (\S\ref{sec:ablation}).
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Execution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.
4	Yet feeding thousands of trace lines to a model can be no better than feeding none: the diagnostic signal drowns in routine execution steps.
5	\emph{Which trace components actually carry the signal that matters?}
6	
7	Prior work found no single trace representation consistently outperforms others \citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks.
8	\emph{Verdict prediction}---classifying whether a test passes or fails---offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, enabling controlled component ablation.
9	As fault detection, the model need not simulate execution, only determine whether something went wrong---and if that signal is concentrated in a small trace subset, information density should matter more than quantity.
10	
11	We test this hypothesis through the first systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.
12	Using 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program}, we decompose a multi-component trace representation (VOTA: exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression) into individual components across four models spanning 1.5B to 13B parameters and three architecture families: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \citep{qwen2025coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \citep{deepseek2024coder}.
13	The results are striking: complete traces (82--88\%) underperform a single compact component (${\geq}$99\%) despite containing strictly more information---a direct demonstration that information density, not quantity, determines learning efficiency.
14	
15	\textbf{Exception events alone achieve ${\geq}$99.83\% verdict accuracy on three of four models (${\geq}$99.33\% on 2/3 seeds for the fourth; one seed reaches 100\%).}
16	DeepSeek-6.7B is the exception: its 3-seed mean is 96.58\% ($\pm$4.76pp) due to one seed exhibiting format collapse (fail recall = 70.5\%), though even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\%) by 6.64pp.
17	This near-perfect accuracy holds from 1.5B to 13B parameters, while the code-only baseline (\texttt{no\_trace}) varies from 67.3\% to 76.5\% with strong scale dependence.
18	Training dynamics expose the mechanism (Figure~\ref{fig:training_dynamics}): \texttt{exception\_only} converges to 99.33\% within 50 training steps and saturates at 99.83\% by step 150, while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates between 82\% and 89\% across the entire training without stable convergence, consistent with overfitting to spurious patterns in verbose content.
19	
20	Adding trace information beyond exceptions provides no benefit and can degrade performance: VOTA (3-seed mean 99.05\%) underperforms \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) on Qwen-7B, with the gap reaching 14.96pp on DeepSeek due to tokenizer-driven truncation.
21	The performance gap approximately decomposes into information overload (${\sim}$76\%) and gradient dilution (${\sim}$24\%) on Qwen-7B (3 seeds).
22	A tail-truncation budget sweep across four models reveals a phase transition at ${\sim}$500 tokens, with \texttt{tail\_2048} recovering 95--98\% of \texttt{exception\_only}'s accuracy.
23	Even on silent logic errors (55.5\% of failures), \texttt{exception\_only} maintains 100\% recall because test assertions generate exception events.
24	
25	Our contributions:
26	\begin{enumerate}
27	\item \textbf{Information density determines SFT learning efficiency}: training dynamics directly show that denser trace components learn faster and more stably---\texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates (82--89\%) without stable convergence. Seed variance negatively correlates with input density ($\sigma{=}$0.00 to 1.12), and SFT closes a 6--49pp zero-shot gap across all models (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
28	\item \textbf{Exception events are the minimal sufficient signal}: ${\geq}$99.83\% on three of four models, ${\geq}$99.33\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds (1.5B--13B, three families), with ${\sim}$91\% semantic rather than format-driven (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}, \S\ref{sec:nomarker}). Cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench ($N{=}426$) preserves the ranking, though margins narrow from ceiling effects (\S\ref{sec:debugbench}).
29	\item \textbf{Approximate density decomposition (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds)}: The performance gap decomposes into gradient dilution (${\sim}$24\%) and information overload (${\sim}$76\%), implying content selection matters more than loss engineering. A budget sweep across four models reveals a phase transition at ${\sim}$500 tail tokens (\S\ref{sec:density}, \S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
30	\item \textbf{Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy (Qwen-7B; cross-model verification in progress)}: Tier~1 signals (exceptions, assertions) individually achieve ${\geq}$99\%; Tier~2 (loop compression) reaches 86.1\%, still +10.9pp over no trace (\S\ref{sec:ablation}).
31	\end{enumerate}
32
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	
3	\subsection{Task Definition}
4	
5	We study \emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.
6	The model produces a single token (\texttt{pass} or \texttt{fail}), making this a binary classification task.
7	Unlike code generation \citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.
8	
9	\subsection{Dataset}
10	\label{sec:dataset}
11	
12	We sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\% pass, 33\% fail).
13	The dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\%).
14	The \texttt{exception\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\% train, 48.99\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).
15	
16	\subsection{Trace Representations}
17	
18	We define seven conditions that vary the trace information available (Figure~\ref{fig:trace_overview}):
19	\begin{itemize}
20	\item \texttt{no\_trace}: Source code and test input only.
21	\item \texttt{full\_trace}: Complete line-by-line trace with variable states.
22	\item \texttt{VOTA}: Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression.
23	\item \texttt{exception\_only}: Only exception/error events.
24	\item \texttt{ae\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected pairs.
25	\item \texttt{loop\_only}: Only loop compression summaries.
26	\item \texttt{VOTA\_no\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information.
27	\end{itemize}
28	
29	\begin{figure*}[t]
30	\centering
31	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}
32	\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \texttt{no\_trace} (75.13\%), \texttt{full\_trace} (81.76\%), VOTA (99.41\%), and \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).}
33	\label{fig:trace_overview}
34	\end{figure*}
35	
36	\subsection{Models and Training}
37	
38	We evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \citep{qwen2025coder}, \textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \citep{roziere2024code}, and \textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\times$).
39	All conditions use LoRA \citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.
40	Sequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.
41	The primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \texttt{exception\_only} robustness (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_details}).
42	
43	\subsection{Loss Ablation}
44	
45	To disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).
46	Improvement over standard \texttt{full\_trace} is attributable to \emph{gradient dilution}; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects \emph{information overload} at inference time.
47	
```
