```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Execution Traces for Code LLMs.}
4	TracePile \citep{chen2025tracepile} scaled trace-augmented training to 2.6M samples; \citet{wang2025dcsh} found trace utility task- and format-dependent, which we extend by identifying exception events as the dominant signal.
5	NExT \citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \citep{eguchi2025etcot} used trace chain-of-thought for repair and execution reasoning; \citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, supporting our density finding.
6	\citet{liu2023codeexecution} pre-trained on execution traces; \citet{chen2024selfdebug} demonstrated LLM self-debugging from execution results.
7	CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, extended to 19 languages \citep{xu2025cruxevalx}, established execution reasoning benchmarks.
8	None decompose which trace components contribute to \emph{classification} accuracy.
9	
10	\paragraph{Code Verdict and Patch Classification.}
11	TRACED \citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; DeepDFA \citep{steenhoek2024deepdfa} and learned program slicing \citep{yadavally2024slicing} combined classical analysis with deep learning.
12	In patch correctness, \citet{tian2020patchcorrectness}, CC2Vec \citep{hoang2020cc2vec}, LLM4PatchCorrect \citep{zhou2024llm4patch} (84.4\%), and APPT \citep{appt2024} (79.7\%) developed representation learning of code changes.
13	Verdict prediction is an instance of the longstanding test oracle problem \citep{barr2015oracle}; oracle generation progressed from ATLAS \citep{watson2020atlas} through retrieval-augmented approaches \citep{yu2022assertion,nashid2023assertion} to TOGA \citep{dinella2022toga}.
14	FuseFL \citep{widyasari2024fusefl} and \citet{foster2025mutation} demonstrated structured fault signals; the equivalent-mutant problem in mutation testing \citep{papadakis2019mutation} poses an analogous classification challenge to verdict prediction on DebugBench's single-point mutations.
15	None isolate individual trace components or compare against rule-based baselines.
16	
17	\paragraph{Fault Localization.}
18	Spectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) ranks program elements by suspiciousness from test spectra \citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar}; recent LLM-based approaches \citep{kang2024autofl,qin2024agentfl,widyasari2024llm4fl,mukherjee2025llmfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.
19	Verdict prediction is a prerequisite to FL (determining \emph{whether} a fault exists before localizing \emph{where}), and both exploit the same signal type at different granularities.
20	
21	\paragraph{Trace Compression and Selection.}
22	TraceCoder \citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \citep{wang2025inspectcoder} use selective trace extraction; RepairAgent \citep{bouzenia2025repairagent} and Agentless \citep{xia2024agentless} incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows, as do SWE-agent \citep{yang2024sweagent} and OpenHands \citep{wang2025openhands}, which close the loop between code generation, execution, and iterative revision.
23	Automated log analysis \citep{he2021loganalysis} provides parsing techniques for extracting structured events from build logs and test outputs, a prerequisite for feeding such signals to downstream models.
24	In the prompt compression literature, LLMLingua \citep{jiang2023llmlingua} and Selective Context \citep{li2023selectivecontext} showed that pruning low-information tokens from LLM inputs preserves downstream accuracy, corroborating our finding that compact trace formats outperform verbose ones.
25	\citet{haque2025traces} found raw traces provide limited repair benefit; SWE-bench \citep{jimenez2024swebench} provides a realistic benchmark.
26	Our approximate decomposition (gradient dilution ${\sim}$24\% vs.\ information overload ${\sim}$76\%) provides the first quantitative framework for \emph{why} compression helps, complementing these methods' focus on \emph{how}.
27	
28	\paragraph{Calibration and Defect Prediction.}
29	Neural network calibration \citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration,spiess2025calibration}, studies confidence-correctness alignment; our ECE ${<}$ 0.007 suggests compact inputs improve calibration.
30	Just-in-time defect prediction \citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features rather than traces.
31	
32	\paragraph{Training Efficiency and Data Selection.}
33	Curriculum learning \citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} show that data quality and ordering improve convergence, consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable rapid convergence while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation.
34	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Fs3Ec2t6eVVP9qwpDo4az9`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	
3	\subsection{Task Definition}
4	
5	We study \emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.
6	The model produces a single token (\texttt{pass} or \texttt{fail}), making this a binary classification task.
7	Unlike code generation \citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.
8	
9	\subsection{Dataset}
10	\label{sec:dataset}
11	
12	We sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\% pass, 33\% fail).
13	The dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\%).
14	The \texttt{exception\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\% train, 48.99\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).
15	
16	\subsection{Trace Representations}
17	
18	We define seven conditions that vary the trace information available (Figure~\ref{fig:trace_overview}):
19	\begin{itemize}
20	\item \texttt{no\_trace}: Source code and test input only.
21	\item \texttt{full\_trace}: Complete line-by-line trace with variable states.
22	\item \texttt{VOTA}: Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression.
23	\item \texttt{exception\_only}: Only exception/error events.
24	\item \texttt{ae\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected pairs.
25	\item \texttt{loop\_only}: Only loop compression summaries.
26	\item \texttt{VOTA\_no\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information.
27	\end{itemize}
28	
29	\begin{figure*}[t]
30	\centering
31	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}
32	\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \texttt{no\_trace} (75.13\%), \texttt{full\_trace} (81.76\%), VOTA (99.41\%), and \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).}
33	\label{fig:trace_overview}
34	\end{figure*}
35	
36	\subsection{Models and Training}
37	
38	We evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \citep{qwen2025coder}, \textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \citep{roziere2024code}, and \textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\times$).
39	All conditions use LoRA \citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.
40	Sequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.
41	The primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \texttt{exception\_only} robustness (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_details}).
42	
43	\subsection{Loss Ablation}
44	
45	To disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).
46	Improvement over standard \texttt{full\_trace} is attributable to \emph{gradient dilution}; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects \emph{information overload} at inference time.
47	
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Definition}\n\nWe study \\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\nThe model produces a single token (\\texttt{pass} or \\texttt{fail}), making this a binary classification task.\nUnlike code generation \\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.\n\n\\subsection{Dataset}\n\\label{sec:dataset}\n\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \\texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\% pass, 33\\% fail).\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\\%).\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\% train, 48.99\\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).\n\n\\subsection{Trace Representations}\n\nWe define seven conditions that vary the trace information available (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\texttt{no\\_trace}: Source code and test input only.\n\\item \\texttt{full\\_trace}: Complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA}: Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression.\n\\item \\texttt{exception\\_only}: Only exception/error events.\n\\item \\texttt{ae\\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected pairs.\n\\item \\texttt{loop\\_only}: Only loop compression summaries.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\n\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%), VOTA (99.41\\%), and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).}\n\\label{fig:trace_overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Training}\n\nWe evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \\citep{qwen2025coder}, \\textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \\citep{roziere2024code}, and \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\\times$).\nAll conditions use LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\nSequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.\nThe primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \\texttt{exception\\_only} robustness (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\n\nTo disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).\nImprovement over standard \\texttt{full\\_trace} is attributable to \\emph{gradient dilution}; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects \\emph{information overload} at inference time.\n",
      "numLines": 47,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 47
    }
  }
}
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Execution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.
4	Yet feeding thousands of trace lines to a model can be no better than feeding none: the diagnostic signal drowns in routine execution steps.
5	\emph{Which trace components actually carry the signal that matters?}
6	
7	Prior work found no single trace representation consistently outperforms others \citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks.
8	\emph{Verdict prediction}---classifying whether a test passes or fails---offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, enabling controlled component ablation.
9	As fault detection, the model need not simulate execution, only determine whether something went wrong---and if that signal is concentrated in a small trace subset, information density should matter more than quantity.
10	
11	We test this hypothesis through the first systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.
12	Using 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program}, we decompose a multi-component trace representation (VOTA: exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression) into individual components across four models spanning 1.5B to 13B parameters and three architecture families: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \citep{qwen2025coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \citep{deepseek2024coder}.
13	The results are striking: complete traces (82--88\%) underperform a single compact component (${\geq}$99\%) despite containing strictly more information---a direct demonstration that information density, not quantity, determines learning efficiency.
14	
15	\textbf{Exception events alone achieve ${\geq}$99.83\% verdict accuracy on three of four models (${\geq}$99.33\% on 2/3 seeds for the fourth; one seed reaches 100\%).}
16	DeepSeek-6.7B is the exception: its 3-seed mean is 96.58\% ($\pm$4.76pp) due to one seed exhibiting format collapse (fail recall = 70.5\%), though even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\%) by 6.64pp.
17	This near-perfect accuracy holds from 1.5B to 13B parameters, while the code-only baseline (\texttt{no\_trace}) varies from 67.3\% to 76.5\% with strong scale dependence.
18	Training dynamics expose the mechanism (Figure~\ref{fig:training_dynamics}): \texttt{exception\_only} converges to 99.33\% within 50 training steps and saturates at 99.83\% by step 150, while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates between 82\% and 89\% across the entire training without stable convergence, consistent with overfitting to spurious patterns in verbose content.
19	
20	Adding trace information beyond exceptions provides no benefit and can degrade performance: VOTA (3-seed mean 99.05\%) underperforms \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) on Qwen-7B, with the gap reaching 14.96pp on DeepSeek due to tokenizer-driven truncation.
21	The performance gap approximately decomposes into information overload (${\sim}$76\%) and gradient dilution (${\sim}$24\%) on Qwen-7B (3 seeds).
22	A tail-truncation budget sweep across four models reveals a phase transition at ${\sim}$500 tokens, with \texttt{tail\_2048} recovering 95--98\% of \texttt{exception\_only}'s accuracy.
23	Even on silent logic errors (55.5\% of failures), \texttt{exception\_only} maintains 100\% recall because test assertions generate exception events.
24	
25	Our contributions:
26	\begin{enumerate}
27	\item \textbf{Information density determines SFT learning efficiency}: training dynamics directly show that denser trace components learn faster and more stably---\texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates (82--89\%) without stable convergence. Seed variance negatively correlates with input density ($\sigma{=}$0.00 to 1.12), and SFT closes a 6--49pp zero-shot gap across all models (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
28	\item \textbf{Exception events are the minimal sufficient signal}: ${\geq}$99.83\% on three of four models, ${\geq}$99.33\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds (1.5B--13B, three families), with ${\sim}$91\% semantic rather than format-driven (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}, \S\ref{sec:nomarker}). Cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench ($N{=}426$) preserves the ranking, though margins narrow from ceiling effects (\S\ref{sec:debugbench}).
29	\item \textbf{Approximate density decomposition (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds)}: The performance gap decomposes into gradient dilution (${\sim}$24\%) and information overload (${\sim}$76\%), implying content selection matters more than loss engineering. A budget sweep across four models reveals a phase transition at ${\sim}$500 tail tokens (\S\ref{sec:density}, \S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
30	\item \textbf{Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy (Qwen-7B; cross-model verification in progress)}: Tier~1 signals (exceptions, assertions) individually achieve ${\geq}$99\%; Tier~2 (loop compression) reaches 86.1\%, still +10.9pp over no trace (\S\ref{sec:ablation}).
31	\end{enumerate}
32
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Execution Traces for Code LLMs.}
4	TracePile \citep{chen2025tracepile} scaled trace-augmented training to 2.6M samples; \citet{wang2025dcsh} found trace utility task- and format-dependent, which we extend by identifying exception events as the dominant signal.
5	NExT \citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \citep{eguchi2025etcot} used trace chain-of-thought for repair and execution reasoning; \citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, supporting our density finding.
6	\citet{liu2023codeexecution} pre-trained on execution traces; \citet{chen2024selfdebug} demonstrated LLM self-debugging from execution results.
7	CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, extended to 19 languages \citep{xu2025cruxevalx}, established execution reasoning benchmarks.
8	None decompose which trace components contribute to \emph{classification} accuracy.
9	
10	\paragraph{Code Verdict and Patch Classification.}
11	TRACED \citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; DeepDFA \citep{steenhoek2024deepdfa} and learned program slicing \citep{yadavally2024slicing} combined classical analysis with deep learning.
12	In patch correctness, \citet{tian2020patchcorrectness}, CC2Vec \citep{hoang2020cc2vec}, LLM4PatchCorrect \citep{zhou2024llm4patch} (84.4\%), and APPT \citep{appt2024} (79.7\%) developed representation learning of code changes.
13	Verdict prediction is an instance of the longstanding test oracle problem \citep{barr2015oracle}; oracle generation progressed from ATLAS \citep{watson2020atlas} through retrieval-augmented approaches \citep{yu2022assertion,nashid2023assertion} to TOGA \citep{dinella2022toga}.
14	FuseFL \citep{widyasari2024fusefl} and \citet{foster2025mutation} demonstrated structured fault signals; the equivalent-mutant problem in mutation testing \citep{papadakis2019mutation} poses an analogous classification challenge to verdict prediction on DebugBench's single-point mutations.
15	None isolate individual trace components or compare against rule-based baselines.
16	
17	\paragraph{Fault Localization.}
18	Spectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) ranks program elements by suspiciousness from test spectra \citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar}; recent LLM-based approaches \citep{kang2024autofl,qin2024agentfl,widyasari2024llm4fl,mukherjee2025llmfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.
19	Verdict prediction is a prerequisite to FL (determining \emph{whether} a fault exists before localizing \emph{where}), and both exploit the same signal type at different granularities.
20	
21	\paragraph{Trace Compression and Selection.}
22	TraceCoder \citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \citep{wang2025inspectcoder} use selective trace extraction; RepairAgent \citep{bouzenia2025repairagent} and Agentless \citep{xia2024agentless} incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows, as do SWE-agent \citep{yang2024sweagent} and OpenHands \citep{wang2025openhands}, which close the loop between code generation, execution, and iterative revision.
23	Automated log analysis \citep{he2021loganalysis} provides parsing techniques for extracting structured events from build logs and test outputs, a prerequisite for feeding such signals to downstream models.
24	In the prompt compression literature, LLMLingua \citep{jiang2023llmlingua} and Selective Context \citep{li2023selectivecontext} showed that pruning low-information tokens from LLM inputs preserves downstream accuracy, corroborating our finding that compact trace formats outperform verbose ones.
25	\citet{haque2025traces} found raw traces provide limited repair benefit; SWE-bench \citep{jimenez2024swebench} provides a realistic benchmark.
26	Our approximate decomposition (gradient dilution ${\sim}$24\% vs.\ information overload ${\sim}$76\%) provides the first quantitative framework for \emph{why} compression helps, complementing these methods' focus on \emph{how}.
27	
28	\paragraph{Calibration and Defect Prediction.}
29	Neural network calibration \citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration,spiess2025calibration}, studies confidence-correctness alignment; our ECE ${<}$ 0.007 suggests compact inputs improve calibration.
30	Just-in-time defect prediction \citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features rather than traces.
31	
32	\paragraph{Training Efficiency and Data Selection.}
33	Curriculum learning \citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} show that data quality and ordering improve convergence, consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable rapid convergence while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation.
34	
```

SWE-bench \\citep{jimenez2024swebench} provides a realistic benchmark.\nOur approximate decomposition (gradient dilution ${\\sim}$24\\% vs.\\ information overload ${\\sim}$76\\%) provides the first quantitative framework for \\emph{why} compression helps, complementing these methods' focus on \\emph{how}.\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration and Defect Prediction.}\nNeural network calibration \\citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \\citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration,spiess2025calibration}, studies confidence-correctness alignment; our ECE ${<}$ 0.007 suggests compact inputs improve calibration.\nJust-in-time defect prediction \\citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features rather than traces.\n\n\\paragraph{Training Efficiency and Data Selection.}\nCurriculum learning \\citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \\citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \\citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} show that data quality and ordering improve convergence, consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable rapid convergence while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation.\n",
```json
      "numLines": 34,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 34
    }
  }
}
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate across two research questions:
4	(1)~Do Tier~1 exception signals generalize across models?
5	(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component?
6	All experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\% CIs.\footnote{Data is partitioned at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; \S\ref{sec:dataset}). The \texttt{exception\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (48.99\% test duplicates; Appendix~\ref{sec:dedup_stats}), but these arise from within-problem variants, not cross-set leakage; deduplication sensitivity analysis confirms 0.00pp delta across all 19 experimental conditions.}
7	Pairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all differences are significant at $p{<}0.0001$ unless noted.
8	
9	\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}
10	\label{sec:cross_model}
11	
12	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning an 8.7$\times$ parameter range (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families (Qwen, DeepSeek, CodeLlama) under key conditions.
13	
14	\begin{table}[t]
15	\centering
16	\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\pm$std over 3 seeds. Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_results}.}
17	\label{tab:cross_model}
18	\small
19	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
20	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
21	\toprule
22	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Qwen-7B} & \textbf{Qwen-1.5B} & \textbf{CL-13B} & \textbf{DS-6.7B} \\
23	\midrule
24	exception\_only & \textbf{99.83}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & \textbf{99.83}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & \textbf{99.89}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.10} & \textbf{99.33}$^\dag$ \\
25	ae\_only & 99.64{\scriptsize$\pm$0.49} & --- & --- & --- \\
26	VOTA & 99.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.38} & 99.58 & 98.57 & 84.45 \\
27	no\_trace & 75.13 & 67.31 & 76.47 & 73.36 \\
28	\midrule
29	Majority baseline & \multicolumn{4}{c}{71.51} \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\end{tabular}%
32	}
33	\vspace{2pt}
34	\par\raggedright\footnotesize
35	$^\dag$DeepSeek 3-seed mean 96.58\%$\pm$4.76pp; seed 123 (91.09\%, fail recall\,=\,70.5\%) exhibits format collapse on long inputs (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}), reported as 2-seed mean excluding the outlier.
36	VOTA on Qwen-7B: 99.41/98.66/99.08 ($\sigma{=}$0.38).
37	\texttt{ae\_only} 3 seeds: 99.92/99.08/99.92 (mean 99.64\%); \texttt{loop\_only}: 85.63/85.88/86.64 (mean 86.05\%).
38	Qwen-1.5B \texttt{exception\_only}: 99.83\% across 3 seeds ($\sigma{=}$0).
39	\end{table}
40	
41	\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}
42	Under \texttt{exception\_only}, the Qwen and CodeLlama families achieve ${\geq}$99.83\% (mean): Qwen-7B (99.83\%$\pm$0.00, 3 seeds), Qwen-1.5B (99.83\%), and CodeLlama-13B (99.89\%$\pm$0.10, 3 seeds; seed 123 achieves 100\%).
43	DeepSeek-6.7B reaches 99.33\% on 2/3 seeds with one LoRA-sensitivity outlier at 91.09\% (seed 123; fail recall = 70.5\%); even the outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\%) by 6.64pp.
44	Excluding the DeepSeek outlier, the cross-model range is 0.56pp (99.33--99.89\%), the tightest of any condition.
45	
46	\paragraph{Scale invariance of exception signals.}
47	Exception-based accuracy is shared by models spanning 1.5B to 13B parameters (8.7$\times$): both Qwen-1.5B and CodeLlama-13B achieve 99.83\%+ on the same representation.
48	CodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\% (0/1190 errors), confirming that Qwen's two persistent errors (from \texttt{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\ref{sec:irreducible}) are high-probability convergence artifacts rather than hard data limitations.
49	In contrast, \texttt{no\_trace} scales monotonically with capacity: 67.31\% (1.5B) $\to$ 75.13\% (7B) $\to$ 76.47\% (13B), a 9.16pp range that exception events completely eliminate (Figure~\ref{fig:cross_scale}).
50	
51	\begin{figure}[t]
52	\centering
53	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}
54	\caption{Verdict accuracy vs.\ model scale. \texttt{exception\_only} maintains ${\geq}$99.33\% regardless of scale (1.5B--13B), while \texttt{no\_trace} shows monotonic scale sensitivity (+9.16pp from 1.5B to 13B). The \texttt{nomarker} condition (format markers removed) shows no degradation at smaller scale (${\sim}$97.7--97.8\%).}
55	\label{fig:cross_scale}
56	\end{figure}
57	
58	\paragraph{VOTA cross-family variation.}
59	VOTA accuracy varies across families: 99.58\% (Qwen-1.5B) $>$ 99.05\%$\pm$0.38 (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds) $>$ 98.57\% (CodeLlama-13B; CI [97.9, 99.2]) $\gg$ 84.45\% (DeepSeek-6.7B).
60	The CodeLlama result (17 errors) narrows the gap: among models without tokenizer-driven truncation, the VOTA range is only 1.01pp (98.57--99.58\%), suggesting that the 14.96pp DeepSeek deficit is primarily a truncation artifact rather than an inherent model capability limitation (91.7\% of DeepSeek training samples were affected; Appendix~\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).
61	In contrast, \texttt{exception\_only} inputs are compact (median 150 tokens, mean 398 due to verbose fail-class exceptions), making the cross-model comparison clean.
62	
63	\subsection{Component Ablation}
64	\label{sec:ablation}
65	
66	We restrict \texttt{ae\_only} and \texttt{loop\_only} ablations to Qwen-7B, as these component-level analyses target the trace composition mechanism rather than cross-model generalization.
67	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B and reveals a two-tier hierarchy on this model; the cross-model consistency of \texttt{exception\_only} (${\geq}$99.33\% on all four models) suggests Tier~1 generalizes, though direct Tier~2 verification on other models remains future work.
68	
69	\begin{table}[t]
70	\centering
71	\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. $^\dag$\texttt{ae\_only}: 3-seed mean 99.64\%$\pm$0.49 (seeds 42/123/456: 99.92/99.08/99.92). \texttt{exception\_only}: 99.83\%$\pm$0.00 (3 seeds). $^\S$VOTA: 3-seed mean 99.05\%$\pm$0.38 (99.41/98.66/99.08). $^\ddag$\texttt{loop\_only}: 3-seed mean 86.05\%$\pm$0.52 (85.63/85.88/86.64). Seed variance negatively correlates with information density: $\sigma{=}$0.00 (exception) ${<}$ 0.38 (VOTA) ${<}$ 0.49 (ae) ${<}$ 0.52 (loop).}
72	\label{tab:ablation}
73	\small
74	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
75	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
76	\toprule
77	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Components} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{Tier} \\
78	\midrule
79	ae\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\
80	exception\_only & exception events & \textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\
81	VOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\
82	VOTA\_no\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\
83	loop\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\
84	full\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\
85	no\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\
86	\midrule
87	Regex (best) & exc.\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\
88	3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\
89	Zero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\
90	\bottomrule
91	\end{tabular}%
92	}
93	\end{table}
94	
95	Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%$\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\%$\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}0.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.
96	Removing assertion points has no effect (\texttt{VOTA\_no\_ae} = 99.41\%, single seed), confirming exception dominance.
97	Tier~2 \texttt{loop\_only} (86.05\%, 3-seed mean) improves 10.92pp over \texttt{no\_trace} ($p{<}0.0001$) but falls far short of Tier~1.
98	\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}
99	The strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching) achieves 93.61\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}0.001$).
100	The gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
101	Across trace formats, SFT consistently outperforms the best applicable regex, from +3.62pp (\texttt{no\_trace} over majority baseline) to +23.86pp (VOTA), with the largest gains on formats requiring semantic interpretation (Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
102	
103	\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}
104	Zero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\% across four models (42.44pp range), with performance uncorrelated with model size (Qwen-1.5B: 93.45\% vs.\ CodeLlama-13B: 51.01\%).
105	Three-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\%) improves only 1.76pp and still trails SFT by 8.99pp.
106	SFT compresses this 42.44pp range to 0.56pp (99.33--99.89\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 training samples (Appendix~\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).
107	
108	\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}
109	\label{sec:nomarker}
110	
111	The \texttt{exception\_only} format includes a \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\% accuracy; Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
112	To quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all format markers removed (\texttt{nomarker} condition).
113	
114	Qwen-7B 3-seed mean: 97.65\% $\pm$ 0.96 (seeds 42/123/456: 96.55/98.32/98.07\%), a drop of 2.18pp from \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%).
115	Qwen-1.5B: 97.82\%, drop of 2.01pp.
116	The marker contribution (${\sim}$2pp) accounts for only ${\sim}$9\% of the total exception lift over \texttt{no\_trace} (24.70pp on 7B).
117	Exception events thus provide two signal layers that show no degradation at smaller scale: structural markers (${\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic exception content (${\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).
118	
119	\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}
120	\label{sec:density}
121	
122	The 17.29pp gap between \texttt{full\_trace} (81.76\%) and VOTA (99.05\%, 3-seed mean) decomposes via \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only} (85.94\%$\pm$1.12, 3 seeds; Table~\ref{tab:density}), which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.
123	
124	\begin{table}[t]
125	\centering
126	\caption{Approximate information density decomposition: gradient dilution (${\sim}$24\%) vs.\ information overload (${\sim}$76\%). \texttt{label\_only} and VOTA are 3-seed means on Qwen-7B.}
127	\label{tab:density}
128	\small
129	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
130	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
131	\toprule
132	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{$\Delta$ vs.\ full\_trace} & \textbf{Gap explained} \\
133	\midrule
134	full\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\
135	label\_only & 85.94{\scriptsize$\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\% \\
136	VOTA & 99.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\% \\
137	\bottomrule
138	\end{tabular}%
139	}
140	\end{table}
141	
142	Gradient dilution accounts for approximately 24\% of the gap (+4.18pp, 3-seed mean): when the model no longer wastes gradient updates on predicting trace tokens, accuracy improves even with identical input.
143	The remaining ${\sim}$76\% (+13.11pp) reflects information overload at inference: the model receives thousands of irrelevant trace lines that compete for attention with the diagnostic signal, regardless of loss design.
144	This asymmetry implies that trace compression methods should prioritize \emph{content selection} (removing irrelevant lines) over \emph{loss engineering} (masking non-target tokens), as the larger bottleneck is what the model sees, not how it is trained.
145	The two factors may interact, so this decomposition is approximate.
146	Label-only loss simultaneously changes multiple optimization dynamics (effective gradient norm, implicit regularization from trace token prediction); the 24\%/76\% attribution should be interpreted as an approximate decomposition rather than a strict causal partition.
147	This analysis uses 3 seeds on a single model (Qwen-7B); cross-model validation remains future work.
148	
149	\subsection{Additional Analyses}
150	\label{sec:additional}
151	
152	\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}
153	\label{sec:trace_length}
154	Varying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\%--81.8\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\%, $p{=}0.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).
155	This non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).
156	
157	\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}
158	Tail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).
159	All four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.
160	
161	A cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a phase transition localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.
162	At tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--82.9\%, only +1.0--6.5pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\to$512 (+11.0--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\sim}$94--98\%).
163	This localizes the critical threshold to ${\sim}$400--500 tokens, matching the typical exception traceback length (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).
164	Beyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\times$seed combinations confirms all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\%, 98.24\%] (Table~\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.
165	\texttt{tail\_2048} captures 95--98\% of \texttt{exception\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.
166	The residual 2--5pp gap confirms that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).
167	
168	\paragraph{Calibration.}
169	\label{sec:calibration}
170	Calibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \texttt{exception\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\ 0.171 for \texttt{no\_trace}, a 63$\times$ gap.
171	This calibration advantage generalizes across models: all three tested architectures achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 under \texttt{exception\_only} (Appendix~\ref{sec:calibration_full}).
172	
173	\paragraph{Error orthogonality.}
174	\texttt{exception\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000), confirming it operates on an independent signal dimension (Appendix~\ref{sec:error_overlap}).
175	
176	\paragraph{Exception type breakdown.}
177	A per-exception-type breakdown (Appendix~\ref{sec:exception_type}) confirms that all standard exception types achieve 100\% accuracy; the only failures arise from \texttt{GeneratorExit}, a benign \texttt{BaseException} subclass.
178	
179	\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}
180	\label{sec:debugbench}
181	On DebugBench \citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the ranking is preserved: \texttt{exception\_only} 100\%, VOTA 99.77\%, \texttt{no\_trace} 96.71\%; narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).
182	
```

</details>

are individually sufficient: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%$\\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}0.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (\\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae} = 99.41\\%, single seed), confirming exception dominance.\nTier~2 \\texttt{loop\\_only} (86.05\\%, 3-seed mean) improves 10.92pp over \\texttt{no\\_trace} ($p{<}0.0001$) but falls far short of Tier~1.\n\\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}\nThe strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching) achieves 93.61\\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}0.001$).\nThe gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \\texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nAcross trace formats, SFT consistently outperforms the best applicable regex, from +3.62pp (\\texttt{no\\_trace} over majority baseline) to +23.86pp (VOTA), with the largest gains on formats requiring semantic interpretation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}\nZero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\\% across four models (42.44pp range), with performance uncorrelated with model size (Qwen-1.5B: 93.45\\% vs.\\ CodeLlama-13B: 51.01\\%).\nThree-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\\%) improves only 1.76pp and still trails SFT by 8.99pp.\nSFT compresses this 42.44pp range to 0.56pp (99.33--99.89\\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 training samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\n\\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}\n\\label{sec:nomarker}\n\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format includes a \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\\% accuracy; Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nTo quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all format markers removed (\\texttt{nomarker} condition).\n\nQwen-7B 3-seed mean: 97.65\\% $\\pm$ 0.96 (seeds 42/123/456: 96.55/98.32/98.07\\%), a drop of 2.18pp from \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%).\nQwen-1.5B: 97.82\\%, drop of 2.01pp.\nThe marker contribution (${\\sim}$2pp) accounts for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the total exception lift over \\texttt{no\\_trace} (24.70pp on 7B).\nException events thus provide two signal layers that show no degradation at smaller scale: structural markers (${\\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic exception content (${\\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe 17.29pp gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) and VOTA (99.05\\%, 3-seed mean) decomposes via \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only} (85.94\\%$\\pm$1.12, 3 seeds; Table~\\ref{tab:density}), which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Approximate information density decomposition: gradient dilution (${\\sim}$24\\%) vs.\\ information overload (${\\sim}$76\\%). \\texttt{label\\_only} and VOTA are 3-seed means on Qwen-7B.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nGradient dilution accounts for approximately 24\\% of the gap (+4.18pp, 3-seed mean): when the model no longer wastes gradient updates on predicting trace tokens, accuracy improves even with identical input.\nThe remaining ${\\sim}$76\\% (+13.11pp) reflects information overload at inference: the model receives thousands of irrelevant trace lines that compete for attention with the diagnostic signal, regardless of loss design.\nThis asymmetry implies that trace compression methods should prioritize \\emph{content selection} (removing irrelevant lines) over \\emph{loss engineering} (masking non-target tokens), as the larger bottleneck is what the model sees, not how it is trained.\nThe two factors may interact, so this decomposition is approximate.\nLabel-only loss simultaneously changes multiple optimization dynamics (effective gradient norm, implicit regularization from trace token prediction); the 24\\%/76\\% attribution should be interpreted as an approximate decomposition rather than a strict causal partition.\nThis analysis uses 3 seeds on a ablation.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nVarying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\\%--81.8\\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\\%, $p{=}0.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic budget curve.}\nTail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).\nAll four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or     "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe evaluate across two research questions:\n(1)~Do Tier~1 exception signals generalize across models?\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component?\nAll experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\\% CIs.\\footnote{Data is partitioned at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; \\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). The \\texttt{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (48.99\\% test duplicates; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}), but these arise from within-problem variants, not cross-set leakage; deduplication sensitivity analysis confirms 0.00pp delta across all 19 experimental conditions.}\nPairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all differences are significant at $p{<}0.0001$ unless noted.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\n\\label{sec:cross_model}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning an 8.7$\\times$ parameter range (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families (Qwen, DeepSeek, CodeLlama) under key conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\\pm$std over 3 seeds. Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:per_class}.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Qwen-7B} & \\textbf{Qwen-1.5B} & \\textbf{CL-13B} & \\textbf{DS-6.7B} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{99.83}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & \\textbf{99.83}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & \\textbf{99.89}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.10} & \\textbf{99.33}$^\\dag$ \\\\\nae\\_only & 99.64{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.49} & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & 99.58 & 98.57 & 84.45 \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13 & 67.31 & 76.47 & 73.36 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{71.51} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\par\\raggedright\\footnotesize\n$^\\dag$DeepSeek 3-seed mean 96.58\\%$\\pm$4.76pp; seed 123 (91.09\\%, fail recall\\,=\\,70.5\\%) exhibits format collapse on long inputs (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}), reported as 2-seed mean excluding the outlier.\nVOTA on Qwen-7B: 99.41/98.66/99.08 ($\\sigma{=}$0.38).\n\\texttt{ae\\_only} 3 seeds: 99.92/99.08/99.92 (mean 99.64\\%); \\texttt{loop\\_only}: 85.63/85.88/86.64 (mean 86.05\\%).\nQwen-1.5B \\texttt{exception\\_only}: 99.83\\% across 3 seeds ($\\sigma{=}$0).\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}\nUnder \\texttt{exception\\_only}, the Qwen and CodeLlama families achieve ${\\geq}$99.83\\% (mean): Qwen-7B (99.83\\%$\\pm$0.00, 3 seeds), Qwen-1.5B (99.83\\%), and CodeLlama-13B (99.89\\%$\\pm$0.10, 3 seeds; seed 123 achieves 100\\%).\nDeepSeek-6.7B reaches 99.33\\% on 2/3 seeds with one LoRA-sensitivity outlier at 91.09\\% (seed 123; fail recall = 70.5\\%); even the outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp.\nExcluding the DeepSeek outlier, the cross-model range is 0.56pp (99.33--99.89\\%), the tightest of any condition.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale invariance of exception signals.}\nException-based accuracy is shared by models spanning 1.5B to 13B parameters (8.7$\\times$): both Qwen-1.5B and CodeLlama-13B achieve 99.83\\%+ on the same representation.\nCodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\\% (0/1190 errors), confirming that Qwen's two persistent errors (from \\texttt{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:irreducible}) are high-probability convergence artifacts rather than hard data limitations.\nIn contrast, \\texttt{no\\_trace} scales monotonically with capacity: 67.31\\% (1.5B) $\\to$ 75.13\\% (7B) $\\to$ 76.47\\% (13B), a 9.16pp range that exception events completely eliminate (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_scale}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Verdict accuracy vs.\\ model scale. \\texttt{exception\\_only} maintains ${\\geq}$99.33\\% regardless of scale (1.5B--13B), while \\texttt{no\\_trace} shows monotonic scale sensitivity (+9.16pp from 1.5B to 13B). The \\texttt{nomarker} condition (format markers removed) shows no degradation at smaller scale (${\\sim}$97.7--97.8\\%).}\n\\label{fig:cross_scale}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{VOTA cross-family variation.}\nVOTA accuracy varies across families: 99.58\\% (Qwen-1.5B) $>$ 99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38 (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds) $>$ 98.57\\% (CodeLlama-13B; CI [97.9, 99.2]) $\\gg$ 84.45\\% (DeepSeek-6.7B).\nThe CodeLlama result (17 errors) narrows the gap: among models without tokenizer-driven truncation, the VOTA range is only 1.01pp (98.57--99.58\\%), suggesting that the 14.96pp DeepSeek deficit is primarily a truncation artifact rather than an inherent model capability limitation (91.7\\% of DeepSeek training samples were affected; Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).\nIn contrast, \\texttt{exception\\_only} inputs are compact (median 150 tokens, mean 398 due to verbose fail-class exceptions), making the cross-model comparison clean.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nWe restrict \\texttt{ae\\_only} and \\texttt{loop\\_only} ablations to Qwen-7B, as these component-level analyses target the trace composition mechanism rather than cross-model generalization.\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B and reveals a two-tier hierarchy on this model; the cross-model consistency of \\texttt{exception\\_only} (${\\geq}$99.33\\% on all four models) suggests Tier~1 generalizes, though direct Tier~2 verification on other models remains future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. $^\\dag$\\texttt{ae\\_only}: 3-seed mean 99.64\\%$\\pm$0.49 (seeds 42/123/456: 99.92/99.08/99.92). \\texttt{exception\\_only}: 99.83\\%$\\pm$0.00 (3 seeds). $^\\S$VOTA: 3-seed mean 99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38 (99.41/98.66/99.08). $^\\ddag$\\texttt{loop\\_only}: 3-seed mean 86.05\\%$\\pm$0.52 (85.63/85.88/86.64). Seed variance negatively correlates with information density: $\\sigma{=}$0.00 (exception) ${<}$ 0.38 (VOTA) ${<}$ 0.49 (ae) ${<}$ 0.52 (loop).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & \\textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nRegex (best) & exc.\\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\\\\n3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\\\\nZero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%$\\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}0.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (\\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae} = 99.41\\%, single seed), confirming exception dominance.\nTier~2 \\texttt{loop\\_only} (86.05\\%, 3-seed mean) improves 10.92pp over \\texttt{no\\_trace} ($p{<}0.0001$) but falls far short of Tier~1.\n\\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}\nThe strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching) achieves 93.61\\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}0.001$).\nThe gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \\texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nAcross trace formats, SFT consistently outperforms the best applicable regex, from +3.62pp (\\texttt{no\\_trace} over majority baseline) to +23.86pp (VOTA), with the largest gains on formats requiring semantic interpretation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}\nZero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\\% across four models (42.44pp range), with performance uncorrelated with model size (Qwen-1.5B: 93.45\\% vs.\\ CodeLlama-13B: 51.01\\%).\nThree-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\\%) improves only 1.76pp and still trails SFT by 8.99pp.\nSFT compresses this 42.44pp range to 0.56pp (99.33--99.89\\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 training samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\n\\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}\n\\label{sec:nomarker}\n\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format includes a \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\\% accuracy; Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nTo quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all format markers removed (\\texttt{nomarker} condition).\n\nQwen-7B 3-seed mean: 97.65\\% $\\pm$ 0.96 (seeds 42/123/456: 96.55/98.32/98.07\\%), a drop of 2.18pp from \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%).\nQwen-1.5B: 97.82\\%, drop of 2.01pp.\nThe marker contribution (${\\sim}$2pp) accounts for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the total exception lift over \\texttt{no\\_trace} (24.70pp on 7B).\nException events thus provide two signal layers that show no degradation at smaller scale: structural markers (${\\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic exception content (${\\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe 17.29pp gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) and VOTA (99.05\\%, 3-seed mean) decomposes via \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only} (85.94\\%$\\pm$1.12, 3 seeds; Table~\\ref{tab:density}), which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Approximate information density decomposition: gradient dilution (${\\sim}$24\\%) vs.\\ information overload (${\\sim}$76\\%). \\texttt{label\\_only} and VOTA are 3-seed means on Qwen-7B.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nGradient dilution accounts for approximately 24\\% of the gap (+4.18pp, 3-seed mean): when the model no longer wastes gradient updates on predicting trace tokens, accuracy improves even with identical input.\nThe remaining ${\\sim}$76\\% (+13.11pp) reflects information overload at inference: the model receives thousands of irrelevant trace lines that compete for attention with the diagnostic signal, regardless of loss design.\nThis asymmetry implies that trace compression methods should prioritize \\emph{content selection} (removing irrelevant lines) over \\emph{loss engineering} (masking non-target tokens), as the larger bottleneck is what the model sees, not how it is trained.\nThe two factors may interact, so this decomposition is approximate.\nLabel-only loss simultaneously changes multiple optimization dynamics (effective gradient norm, implicit regularization from trace token prediction); the 24\\%/76\\% attribution should be interpreted as an approximate decomposition rather than a strict causal partition.\nThis analysis uses 3 seeds on a single model (Qwen-7B); cross-model validation remains future work.\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nVarying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\\%--81.8\\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\\%, $p{=}0.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\nThis non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).\n\n\\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}\nTail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).\nAll four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.\n\nA cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a phase transition localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.\nAt tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--82.9\\%, only +1.0--6.5pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\\to$512 (+11.0--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\\sim}$94--98\\%).\nThis localizes the critical threshold to ${\\sim}$400--500 tokens, matching the typical exception traceback length (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).\nBeyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\\times$seed combinations confirms all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\\%, 98.24\\%] (Table~\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.\n\\texttt{tail\\_2048} captures 95--98\\% of \\texttt{exception\\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.\nThe residual 2--5pp gap confirms that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\\ 0.171 for \\texttt{no\\_trace}, a 63$\\times$ gap.\nThis calibration advantage generalizes across models: all three tested architectures achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 under \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Appendix~\\ref{sec:calibration_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error orthogonality.}\n\\texttt{exception\\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000), confirming it operates on an independent signal dimension (Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_overlap}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exception type breakdown.}\nA per-exception-type breakdown (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exception_type}) confirms that all standard exception types achieve 100\\% accuracy; the only failures arise from \\texttt{GeneratorExit}, a benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclass.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nOn DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the ranking is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\texttt{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%; narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n",

Appendix~\\ref{sec:irreducible}) are high-probability convergence artifacts rather than hard data limitations.\nIn contrast, \\texttt{no\\_trace} scales monotonically with capacity: 67.31\\% (1.5B) $\\to$ 75.13\\% (7B) $\\to$ 76.47\\% (13B), a 9.16pp range that exception events completely eliminate (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_scale}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Verdict accuracy vs.\\ model scale. \\texttt{exception\\_only} maintains ${\\geq}$99.33\\% regardless of scale (1.5B--13B), while \\texttt{no\\_trace} shows monotonic scale sensitivity (+9.16pp from 1.5B to 13B). The \\texttt{nomarker} condition (format markers removed) shows no degradation at smaller scale (${\\sim}$97.7--97.8\\%).}\n\\label{fig:cross_scale}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{VOTA cross-family variation.}\nVOTA accuracy varies across families: 99.58\\% (Qwen-1.5B) $>$ 99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38 (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds) $>$ 98.57\\% (CodeLlama-13B; CI [97.9, 99.2]) $\\gg$ 84.45\\% (DeepSeek-6.7B).\nThe CodeLlama result (17 errors) narrows the gap: among models without tokenizer-driven truncation, the VOTA range is only 1.01pp (98.57--99.58\\%), suggesting that the 14.96pp DeepSeek deficit is primarily a truncation artifact rather than an inherent model capability limitation (91.7\\% of DeepSeek training samples were affected; Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).\nIn contrast, \\texttt{exception\\_only} inputs are compact (median 150 tokens, mean 398 due to verbose fail-class exceptions), making the cross-model comparison clean.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nWe restrict \\texttt{ae\\_only} and \\texttt{loop\\_only} ablations to Qwen-7B, as these component-level analyses target the trace composition mechanism rather than cross-model generalization.\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B and reveals a two-tier hierarchy on this model; the cross-model consistency of \\texttt{exception\\_only} (${\\geq}$99.33\\% on all four models) suggests Tier~1 generalizes, though direct Tier~2 verification on other models remains future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. $^\\dag$\\texttt{ae\\_only}: 3-seed mean 99.64\\%$\\pm$0.49 (seeds 42/123/456: 99.92/99.08/99.92). \\texttt{exception\\_only}: 99.83\\%$\\pm$0.00 (3 seeds). $^\\S$VOTA: 3-seed mean 99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38 (99.41/98.66/99.08). $^\\ddag$\\texttt{loop\\_only}: 3-seed mean 86.05\\%$\\pm$0.52 (85.63/85.88/86.64). Seed variance negatively correlates with information density: $\\sigma{=}$0.00 (exception) ${<}$ 0.38 (VOTA) ${<}$ 0.49 (ae) ${<}$ 0.52 (loop).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & \\textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nRegex (best) & exc.\\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\\\\n3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\\\\nZero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%$\\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}0.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (\\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae} = 99.41\\%, single seed), confirming exception dominance.\nTier~2 \\texttt{loop\\_only} (86.05\\%, 3-seed mean) improves 10.92pp over \\texttt{no\\_trace} ($p{<}0.0001$) but falls far short of Tier~1.\n\\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}\nThe strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching; Table~\\ref{tab:regex}) achieves 93.61\\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}0.001$).\nThe gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \\texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nAcross trace formats, SFT consistently outperforms the best applicable regex, from +3.62pp (\\texttt{no\\_trace} over majority baseline) to +23.86pp (VOTA), with the largest gains on formats requiring semantic interpretation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}\nZero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\\% across four models (42.44pp range), with performance uncorrelated with model size (Qwen-1.5B: 93.45\\% vs.\\ CodeLlama-13B: 51.01\\%).\nThree-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\\%) improves only 1.76pp and still trails SFT by 8.99pp.\nSFT compresses this 42.44pp range to 0.56pp (99.33--99.89\\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 training samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\n\\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}\n\\label{sec:nomarker}\n\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format includes a \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\\% accuracy; Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nTo quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all format markers removed (\\texttt{nomarker} condition).\n\nQwen-7B 3-seed mean: 97.65\\% $\\pm$ 0.96 (seeds 42/123/456: 96.55/98.32/98.07\\%), a drop of 2.18pp from \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%).\nQwen-1.5B: 97.82\\%, drop of 2.01pp.\nThe marker contribution (${\\sim}$2pp) accounts for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the total exception lift over \\texttt{no\\_trace} (24.70pp on 7B).\nException events thus provide two signal layers that show no degradation at smaller scale: structural markers (${\\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic exception content (${\\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe 17.29pp gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) and VOTA (99.05\\%, 3-seed mean) decomposes via \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only} (85.94\\%$\\pm$1.12, 3 seeds; Table~\\ref{tab:density}), which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Approximate information density decomposition: gradient dilution (${\\sim}$24\\%) vs.\\ information overload (${\\sim}$76\\%). \\texttt{label\\_only} and VOTA are 3-seed means on Qwen-7B.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nGradient dilution accounts for approximately 24\\% of the gap (+4.18pp, 3-seed mean): when the model no longer wastes gradient updates on predicting trace tokens, accuracy improves even with identical input.\nThe remaining ${\\sim}$76\\% (+13.11pp) reflects information overload at inference: the model receives thousands of irrelevant trace lines that compete for attention with the diagnostic signal, regardless of loss design.\nThis asymmetry implies that trace compression methods should prioritize \\emph{content selection} (removing irrelevant lines) over \\emph{loss engineering} (masking non-target tokens), as the larger bottleneck is what the model sees, not how it is trained.\nThe two factors may interact, so this decomposition is approximate.\nLabel-only loss simultaneously changes multiple optimization dynamics (effective gradient norm, implicit regularization from trace token prediction); the 24\\%/76\\% attribution should be interpreted as an approximate decomposition rather than a strict causal partition.\nThis analysis uses 3 seeds on a single model (Qwen-7B); cross-model validation remains future work.\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nVarying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\\%--81.8\\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\\%, $p{=}0.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\nThis non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).\n\n\\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}\nTail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).\nAll four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.\n\nA cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a phase transition localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.\nAt tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--82.9\\%, only +1.0--6.5pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\\to$512 (+11.0--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\\sim}$94--98\\%).\nThis localizes the critical threshold to ${\\sim}$400--500 tokens, matching the typical exception traceback length (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).\nBeyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\\times$seed combinations confirms all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\\%, 98.24\\%] (Table~\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.\n\\texttt{tail\\_2048} captures 95--98\\% of \\texttt{exception\\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.\nThe residual 2--5pp gap confirms that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\\ 0.171 for \\texttt{no\\_trace}, a 63$\\times$ gap.\nThis calibration advantage generalizes across models: all three tested architectures achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 under \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Appendix~\\ref{sec:calibration_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error orthogonality.}\n\\texttt{exception\\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000), confirming it operates on an independent signal dimension (Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_overlap}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exception type breakdown.}\nA per-exception-type breakdown (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exception_type}) confirms that all standard exception types achieve 100\\% accuracy; the only failures arise from \\texttt{GeneratorExit}, a benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclass.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nOn DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the ranking is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\texttt{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%; narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n",
