The \\texttt{nomarker} condition (format markers removed) shows no degradation at smaller scale (${\\sim}$97.7--97.8\\%).}\n\\label{fig:cross_scale}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{VOTA cross-family variation.}\nVOTA accuracy varies across families: 99.58\\% (Qwen-1.5B) $>$ 99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38 (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds) $>$ 98.57\\% (CodeLlama-13B; CI [97.9, 99.2]) $\\gg$ 84.45\\% (DeepSeek-6.7B).\nThe CodeLlama result (17 errors) narrows the gap: among models without tokenizer-driven truncation, the VOTA range is only 1.01pp (98.57--99.58\\%), suggesting that the 14.96pp DeepSeek deficit is primarily a truncation artifact rather than an inherent model capability limitation (91.7\\% of DeepSeek training samples were affected; Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).\nIn contrast, \\texttt{exception\\_only} inputs are compact (median 150 tokens, mean 398 due to verbose fail-class exceptions), making the cross-model comparison clean (Appendix~\\ref{sec:robustness_analysis}).\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nWe restrict \\texttt{ae\\_only} and \\texttt{loop\\_only} ablations to Qwen-7B, as these component-level analyses target the trace composition mechanism rather than cross-model generalization.\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B and reveals a two-tier hierarchy on this model; the cross-model consistency of \\texttt{exception\\_only} (${\\geq}$99.33\\% on all four models) suggests Tier~1 generalizes, though direct Tier~2 verification on other models remains future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. $^\\dag$\\texttt{ae\\_only}: 3-seed mean 99.64\\%$\\pm$0.49 (seeds 42/123/456: 99.92/99.08/99.92). \\texttt{exception\\_only}: 99.83\\%$\\pm$0.00 (3 seeds). $^\\S$VOTA: 3-seed mean 99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38 (99.41/98.66/99.08). $^\\ddag$\\texttt{loop\\_only}: 3-seed mean 86.05\\%$\\pm$0.52 (85.63/85.88/86.64). Seed variance negatively correlates with information density: $\\sigma{=}$0.00 (exception) ${<}$ 0.38 (VOTA) ${<}$ 0.49 (ae) ${<}$ 0.52 (loop).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & \\textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nRegex (best) & exc.\\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\\\\n3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\\\\nZero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%$\\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\\%$\\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}0.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (\\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae} = 99.41\\%, single seed), confirming exception dominance.\nTier~2 \\texttt{loop\\_only} (86.05\\%, 3-seed mean) improves 10.92pp over \\texttt{no\\_trace} ($p{<}0.0001$) but falls far short of Tier~1.\n\\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}\nThe strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching; Table~\\ref{tab:regex}) achieves 93.61\\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}0.001$).\nThe gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \\texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nAcross trace formats, SFT consistently outperforms the best applicable regex, from +3.62pp (\\texttt{no\\_trace} over majority baseline) to +23.86pp (VOTA), with the largest gains on formats requiring semantic interpretation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}\nZero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\\% across four models (42.44pp range), with performance uncorrelated with model size (Qwen-1.5B: 93.45\\% vs.\\ CodeLlama-13B: 51.01\\%).\nThree-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\\%) improves only 1.76pp and still trails SFT by 8.99pp.\nSFT compresses this 42.44pp range to 0.56pp (99.33--99.89\\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 training samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\n\\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}\n\\label{sec:nomarker}\n\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format includes a \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\\% accuracy; Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nTo quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all format markers removed (\\texttt{nomarker} condition).\n\nQwen-7B 3-seed mean: 97.65\\% $\\pm$ 0.96 (seeds 42/123/456: 96.55/98.32/98.07\\%), a drop of 2.18pp from \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%).\nQwen-1.5B: 97.82\\%, drop of 2.01pp.\nThe marker contribution (${\\sim}$2pp) accounts for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the total exception lift over \\texttt{no\\_trace} (24.70pp on 7B).\nException events thus provide two signal layers that show no degradation at smaller scale: structural markers (${\\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic exception content (${\\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe 17.29pp gap between \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) and VOTA (99.05\\%, 3-seed mean) decomposes via \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only} (85.94\\%$\\pm$1.12, 3 seeds; Table~\\ref{tab:density}), which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Approximate information density decomposition: gradient dilution (${\\sim}$24\\%) vs.\\ information overload (${\\sim}$76\\%). \\texttt{label\\_only} and VOTA are 3-seed means on Qwen-7B.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nGradient dilution accounts for approximately 24\\% of the gap (+4.18pp, 3-seed mean): when the model no longer wastes gradient updates on predicting trace tokens, accuracy improves even with identical input.\nThe remaining ${\\sim}$76\\% (+13.11pp) reflects information overload at inference: the model receives thousands of irrelevant trace lines that compete for attention with the diagnostic signal, regardless of loss design.\nThis asymmetry implies that trace compression methods should prioritize \\emph{content selection} (removing irrelevant lines) over \\emph{loss engineering} (masking non-target tokens), as the larger bottleneck is what the model sees, not how it is trained.\nThe two factors may interact, so this decomposition is approximate.\nLabel-only loss simultaneously changes multiple optimization dynamics (effective gradient norm, implicit regularization from trace token prediction); the 24\\%/76\\% attribution should be interpreted as an approximate decomposition rather than a strict causal partition.\nThis analysis uses 3 seeds on a single model (Qwen-7B); cross-model validation remains future work.\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nVarying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\\%--81.8\\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\\%, $p{=}0.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\nThis non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).\n\n\\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}\nTail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).\nAll four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.\n\nA cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a phase transition localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.\nAt tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--82.9\\%, only +1.0--6.5pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\\to$512 (+11.0--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\\sim}$94--98\\%).\nThis localizes the critical threshold to ${\\sim}$400--500 tokens, matching the typical exception traceback length (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).\nBeyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\\times$seed combinations confirms all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\\%, 98.24\\%] (Table~\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.\n\\texttt{tail\\_2048} captures 95--98\\% of \\texttt{exception\\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.\nThe residual 2--5pp gap confirms that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\\ 0.171 for \\texttt{no\\_trace}, a 63$\\times$ gap.\nThis calibration advantage generalizes across models: all three tested architectures achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 under \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Appendix~\\ref{sec:calibration_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error orthogonality.}\n\\texttt{exception\\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000), confirming it operates on an independent signal dimension (Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_overlap}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exception type breakdown.}\nA per-exception-type breakdown (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exception_type}) confirms that all standard exception types achieve 100\\% accuracy; the only failures arise from \\texttt{GeneratorExit}, a benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclass.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nOn DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the ranking is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\texttt{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%; narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "49fbbf16-f155-47fe-bb0e-97dfb8a21ad2",

  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "\\item \\texttt{VOTA}: Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression.",
    "newString": "\\item \\texttt{VOTA}: Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression (Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Definition}\n\nWe study \\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\nThe model produces a single token (\\texttt{pass} or \\texttt{fail}), making this a binary classification task.\nUnlike code generation \\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.\nThe discrete binary outcome makes the effect of each trace component directly measurable, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks where output quality varies continuously.\n\n\\subsection{Dataset}\n\\label{sec:dataset}\n\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \\texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\% pass, 33\\% fail).\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\\%).\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\% train, 48.99\\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).\n\n\\subsection{Trace Representations}\n\nWe define seven conditions that vary the trace information available (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\texttt{no\\_trace}: Source code and test input only.\n\\item \\texttt{full\\_trace}: Complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA}: Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression.\n\\item \\texttt{exception\\_only}: Only exception/error events.\n\\item \\texttt{ae\\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected pairs.\n\\item \\texttt{loop\\_only}: Only loop compression summaries.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\n\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%), VOTA (99.41\\%), and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).}\n\\label{fig:trace_overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Training}\n\nWe evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \\citep{qwen2025coder}, \\textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \\citep{roziere2024code}, and \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\\times$).\nAll conditions use LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\nSequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.\nThe primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \\texttt{exception\\_only} robustness (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\n\nTo disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).\nImprovement over standard \\texttt{full\\_trace} is attributable to \\emph{gradient dilution}; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects \\emph{information overload} at inference time.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "49fbbf16-f155-47fe-bb0e-97dfb8a21ad2",

  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).}",
    "newString": "Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$). Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_examples} provides input/output examples.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Definition}\n\nWe study \\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\nThe model produces a single token (\\texttt{pass} or \\texttt{fail}), making this a binary classification task.\nUnlike code generation \\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.\nThe discrete binary outcome makes the effect of each trace component directly measurable, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks where output quality varies continuously.\n\n\\subsection{Dataset}\n\\label{sec:dataset}\n\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \\texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\% pass, 33\\% fail).\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\\%).\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\% train, 48.99\\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).\n\n\\subsection{Trace Representations}\n\nWe define seven conditions that vary the trace information available (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\texttt{no\\_trace}: Source code and test input only.\n\\item \\texttt{full\\_trace}: Complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA}: Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression (Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).\n\\item \\texttt{exception\\_only}: Only exception/error events.\n\\item \\texttt{ae\\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected pairs.\n\\item \\texttt{loop\\_only}: Only loop compression summaries.\n\\item \\texttt{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\n\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%), VOTA (99.41\\%), and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).}\n\\label{fig:trace_overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Training}\n\nWe evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \\citep{qwen2025coder}, \\textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \\citep{roziere2024code}, and \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\\times$).\nAll conditions use LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\nSequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.\nThe primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \\texttt{exception\\_only} robustness (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\n\nTo disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \\texttt{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).\nImprovement over standard \\texttt{full\\_trace} is attributable to \\emph{gradient dilution}; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects \\emph{information overload} at inference time.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 30,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 30,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\begin{figure*}[t]",
          " \\centering",
          " \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}",
          "-\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%), VOTA (99.41\\%), and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).}",
          "+\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%), \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%), VOTA (99.41\\%), and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$). Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_examples} provides input/output examples.}",
          " \\label{fig:trace_overview}",
